Qubit measurement and backaction in a multimode nonreciprocal system
B. T. Miller, F. Lecocq, L. Orr 1,∗ and A. Metelmann 1,2,3,

Abstract
High fidelity qubit readout is a cornerstone for quantum information protocols. In traditional superconducting qubit readout, a chain of microwave amplifiers and nonreciprocal components aid in detecting the qubit’s state with tolerable added noise and backaction. However, the loss, size, and magnetic field of standard nonreciprocal components have sparked a decades-long search for more efficient and scalable alternatives. One prominent approach employs networks of parametrically coupled modes to achieve nonreciprocity. While this class of devices can be directly integrated with the qubit’s readout cavity, current understanding of the resulting single quantum system is substantially lacking. Here we provide a first-principles theoretical tool to understand and design networks of linear modes integrated with embedded qubits. We utilize this theory to inform and analyze the experimental implementation of a qubit readout with an integrated three-mode nonreciprocal system. In doing so, we achieve excellent agreement between the experimental and theoretical qubit measurement and dephasing rates. We then theoretically analyze the same system operated as an integrated nonreciprocal amplifier, predicting high efficiency for reasonable experimental parameters.



pacs

84.30.Le, 03.65.Ta, 42.50.Pq, 42.50.Lc

I
Introduction

Qubit measurement is a fundamental requirement of quantum computation and sensing protocols. In superconducting systems, qubit information is typically encoded in a weak microwave field that is sent through a series of amplifiers and circulators. The amplifiers aid in preserving the measurement signal-to-noise ratio (SNR) throughout the readout chain, while the circulators directionally route signals and suppress amplifier backaction. However, the commercially-available ferrite circulators commonly used have a large physical footprint and operate with a strong magnetic field. This hinders their integrability with superconducting circuits, and thus the scalability of superconducting quantum processors and sensors. In addition, their intrinsic and associated wiring losses are currently the dominant sources of measurement inefficiency.

These issues have inspired a decades-long search for low-loss, compact alternatives to ferrite circulators which operate with small magnetic fields. These alternatives have utilized mixing and delay lines
Kamal et al. 2011
;
Chapman et al. 2017
;
Rosenthal et al. 2017
, on-chip switches
Chapman et al. 2019
, loops of DC voltage-biased Josephson junctions
Navarathna et al. 2023
;
Fedorov et al. 2024
, pumped nonlinear transmission lines
Ranzani et al. 2017
, and networks of parametrically coupled modes
Estep et al. 2014
;
Ranzani and Aumentado 2015
;
Metelmann and Clerk 2015
;
Kerckhoff et al. 2015
;
Sliwa et al. 2015
;
Peterson et al. 2019
;
Naaman and Aumentado 2022
;
Kwende et al. 2023
;
Bello et al. 2026
. Several of these strategies have been extended to create a parametric amplifier with reverse isolation
Lecocq et al. 2017
;
Lecocq et al. 2020
;
Malnou et al. 2025
;
Ranadive et al. 2025
;
Kow and Bell 2025
, facilitating high-fidelity, high-efficiency, and low-backaction qubit measurement without ferrite circulators at the amplifier input
Abdo et al. 2014
;
Abdo et al. 2021
;
Rosenthal et al. 2021
;
Lecocq et al. 2021
. Among the various schemes to achieve this integrated readout, networks of parametrically coupled linear modes currently exhibit the lowest backaction while maintaining record efficiency. However, the quantitative understanding of qubit measurement and backaction in these networks is rudimentary, presenting a hurdle to the optimization of their design.

Historically, the presence of ferrite circulators between the qubit’s readout cavity and parametric amplifier have allowed for their independent design and optimization. However, in the absence of intermediate isolation, these two components become a single quantum system, conceptually represented in Fig.
1
. As a consequence, the amplifier becomes qubit-state dependent, and conversely, the qubit’s evolution is directly affected by the state of the amplifier
Eddins et al. 2019
. In contrast with previous analyses of multimode networks, we present here a full theory that captures this interdependence. We leverage this theory to design and experimentally operate a system consisting of a qubit coupled to a three-mode readout network.

This paper is organized as follows. In Sec.
II
, we introduce a general method for computing the measurement efficiency for an arbitrary linear readout network, emphasizing the phase-space method to compute the qubit’s steady-state dephasing rate. In Sec.
III
, we apply general principles from the theory to motivate our experimental implementation of a three-mode readout network. In Sec.
IV
, we utilize the theory to experimentally extract the thermal occupancy of each mode comprising the network. We then experimentally demonstrate the nonreciprocal routing of signals and noise in the readout network, extracting qubit measurement and dephasing rates that quantitatively agree with theory. Lastly, we theoretically examine the device’s operation as an embedded amplifier by including parametric gain. We thereby show that this device serves not only as a test-bed for the theory presented in this work, but also as a promising step toward higher efficiency qubit measurement.

Figure 1:
Integrating the readout network
.
(a)
Conceptual diagram of a dispersive superconducting qubit measurement with a traditional readout chain consisting of a qubit (Q) and cavity (C) subsystem
\hat{\rho}_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{Q}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{Q}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{Q}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{Q}$}},\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{C}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{C}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{C}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{C}$}}}
, as well as an arbitrary resonant parametric amplifier
\hat{\rho}_{\mathrm{amp}}
, separated by an isolator. The amplifier is comprised of a network of coupled modes labeled
\mathrm{M}_{n}
,
n\in\mathbb{N}
.
(b)
When the intermediate isolator is removed, the cavity mode must be included in the network of modes comprising the amplifier. The qubit and readout network therefore combine into a single quantum system described by the inseparable density matrix
\hat{\rho}_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{Q}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{Q}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{Q}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{Q}$}},\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{C}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{C}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{C}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{C}$}},\mathrm{amp}}
.

II
General Theory

To quantitatively understand the detection of a qubit state, the measurement rate can be related to the network’s scattering matrix, which can be computed with existing methods
Ranzani and Aumentado 2015
;
Lecocq et al. 2017
;
Lecocq et al. 2020
. In contrast, to accurately compute qubit backaction, one must model all components of the readout network’s internal state which couple to the qubit. The presence of multiple modes and gain requires a high-dimensional truncated Hilbert space to represent the system state when numerically solving the full master equation, resulting in an expensive computation. To render the calculation of the qubit backaction tractable, we expand on a phase-space method akin to those presented in Refs.
Gambetta et al. 2006
;
Clerk and Utami 2007
;
Eddins et al. 2019
. The key to this method is the assumption that all states within the network remain Gaussian, which greatly reduces the degrees of freedom in the model. This allows one to quantify the backaction of the readout network on the qubit with a quick numerical, or in some cases, analytical computation.

II.1
Background

A quantum-nondemolition (QND) measurement
Braginsky et al. 1980
of the state of a qubit involves the coherent coupling of the qubit observable
\hat{\sigma}_{z}=\ket{e}\!\bra{e}-\ket{g}\!\bra{g}
to a meter.
Ideally, the only source of incoherent measurement backaction are from fluctuations in the meter, which result in fluctuations of the qubit transition frequency, and hence qubit decoherence in the form of dephasing. We will start with a review of how this manifests in the evolution of the single qubit state, and then connect this to the well-known case of a dispersive measurement with a single linear mode. First, we expand the density operator for the total system in the basis of the measured qubit,

\hat{\rho}=\hat{\rho}_{ee}\ket{e}\!\bra{e}+\hat{\rho}_{gg}\ket{g}\!\bra{g}+\hat{\rho}_{eg}\ket{e}\!\bra{g}+\hat{\rho}_{ge}\ket{g}\!\bra{e},

(1)

where the operators
\hat{\rho}_{jk}
act on the Hilbert space of the meter system. The loss of qubit coherence through dephasing will result in the decay of the magnitude of
\hat{\rho}_{eg}
, thereby reducing the purity of
\hat{\rho}
over time. Modeling the backaction on the qubit state therefore requires solving the dynamics of the meter, and then tracing over the meter’s degrees of freedom,
{\mathrm{Tr}}_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{R}$}}}[\hat{\rho}_{eg}(t)]
.

The master equation governing the dynamics of the total system is described using a superoperator
\mathcal{L}
. We assume that the meter is only coherently coupled to the qubit transition operator
\hat{\sigma}_{z}
, and that
\mathcal{L}
corresponds to a Lindblad master equation. Consequently, taking the trace with respect to the meter,
{\mathrm{Tr}}_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{R}$}}}[\mathcal{L}(\hat{\rho})]
, yields the following reduced master equation
Gambetta et al. 2008
for the qubit density operator,
\hat{\rho}_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{Q}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{Q}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{Q}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{Q}$}}}={\mathrm{Tr}}_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{R}$}}}[\hat{\rho}]
,

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}\hat{\rho}_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{Q}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{Q}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{Q}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{Q}$}}}=

\displaystyle\,-i\frac{\tilde{\omega}_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{Q}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{Q}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{Q}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{Q}$}}}+B(t)}{2}[{\hat{\sigma}_{z}},{\hat{\rho}_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{Q}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{Q}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{Q}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{Q}$}}}}]+\frac{\Gamma_{\varphi}+\Gamma_{\mathrm{d}}(t)}{2}\mathcal{D}[\hat{\sigma}_{z}](\hat{\rho}_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{Q}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{Q}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{Q}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{Q}$}}})

\displaystyle\,+\Gamma_{-}\mathcal{D}[\hat{\sigma}_{-}](\hat{\rho}_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{Q}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{Q}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{Q}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{Q}$}}})+\Gamma_{+}\mathcal{D}[\hat{\sigma}_{+}](\hat{\rho}_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{Q}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{Q}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{Q}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{Q}$}}}),

(2)

where
\mathcal{D}[\hat{o}](\hat{\rho})=\hat{o}\hat{\rho}\hat{o}^{\dagger}-\frac{1}{2}\{{\hat{o}^{\dagger}\hat{o}},{\hat{\rho}}\}
is the dissipation super-operator. Here, we also account for the coupling between the qubit and the environment, leading to the Lamb-shifted qubit transition frequency
\tilde{\omega}_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{Q}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{Q}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{Q}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{Q}$}}}
, pure qubit dephasing with rate
\Gamma_{\varphi}
, and the energy relaxation and excitation rates
\Gamma_{\mp}
.

The backaction on the qubit from the meter is encapsulated in the induced dephasing rate
\Gamma_{\mathrm{d}}(t)
and the frequency shift
B(t)
. Given that
{\mathrm{Tr}}_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{R}$}}}[\hat{\rho}_{eg}(t)]=\braket{e|\hat{\rho}_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{Q}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{Q}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{Q}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{Q}$}}}(t)|g}
, a connection can be established between the state of the meter and the decoherence of the qubit state modeled by the reduced master equation from above,

\displaystyle{\mathrm{Tr}}_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{R}$}}}[\hat{\rho}_{eg}(t)]=

\displaystyle\,e^{-t\left(i\tilde{\omega}_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{Q}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{Q}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{Q}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{Q}$}}}+\Gamma_{2}\right)}

(3)

\displaystyle\times e^{-\int^{t}_{0}\left(iB(\tau)+\Gamma_{\mathrm{d}}(\tau)\right)d\tau}\,{\mathrm{Tr}}_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{R}$}}}[\hat{\rho}_{eg}(0)],

where
\Gamma_{2}=\Gamma_{\varphi}+(\Gamma_{+}+\Gamma_{-})/2
is the innate qubit decoherence rate. From this expression, it is evident that, in the presence of dephasing,
\left\lvert{{\mathrm{Tr}}_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{R}$}}}[\hat{\rho}_{eg}(t)]}\right\rvert\neq\left\lvert{{\mathrm{Tr}}_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{R}$}}}[\hat{\rho}_{eg}(0)]}\right\rvert
, and so the trace of
\hat{\rho}_{eg}
is not conserved.

Hence, in order to simulate the dynamics of
\Gamma_{\mathrm{d}}(t)
and
B(t)
we must generate tractable equations of motion for
{\mathrm{Tr}}_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{R}$}}}[\hat{\rho}_{eg}(t)]
. For various implementations of a single linear mode dispersively coupled to a qubit, this has been done using a phase space quasi-probability distribution (QPD) for
\hat{\rho}_{eg}(t)

Gambetta et al. 2006
;
Serban et al. 2007
;
Clerk and Utami 2007
;
Eddins et al. 2019
. These methods transform the master equation for
\hat{\rho}_{eg}(t)
into a partial differential equation (PDE) for the associated QPD, whose dynamics are then solved.

While the choice of representation is arbitrary, herein we will use the Wigner phase space QPD for
\hat{\rho}_{eg}(t)
, which will be denoted
W_{eg}(q,p;t)
for a meter which is comprised of a single linear mode, where
q
and
p
are the mode’s phase-space coordinates. The trace of
\hat{\rho}_{eg}(t)
is then obtained by integrating the Wigner QPD over the entire phase space,

{\mathrm{Tr}}_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{R}$}}}[\hat{\rho}_{eg}(t)]\equiv\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}W_{eg}(q,p;t)\,dq\,dp=e^{-\nu(t)}.

(4)

The parameter
\exp[-\nu(t)]
corresponds to the zeroth order moment of
W_{eg}(q,p;t)
. Using Eq. (
3
), we can infer that
\nu(t)
contains all the information about the induced dephasing and frequency shift on the qubit:

\Gamma_{\mathrm{d}}(t)=\frac{d}{dt}\real\left[\nu(t)\right]\qquad B(t)=\frac{d}{dt}\mathrm{Im}\left[\nu(t)\right].

(5)

Here, the intrinsic qubit dynamics in the
e^{-t\left(i\tilde{\omega}_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{Q}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{Q}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{Q}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{Q}$}}}+\Gamma_{2}\right)}
term has been removed from the evolution of
W_{eg}(q,p;t)
. In practice, the induced frequency shift on the qubit,
B(t)
, may be eliminated by a unitary transformation, and as a result, in this work we will focus solely on the dephasing from the coupled meter,
\Gamma_{\mathrm{d}}(t)
as it is responsible for irreversible loss of qubit information. Provided that qubit and single mode meter are only dispersively coupled,
\chi\hat{a}^{\dagger}\hat{a}\hat{\sigma}_{z}
where the single mode operators obey
[{\hat{a}},{\hat{a}^{\dagger}}]=1
, and the state of the linear mode is Gaussian, then
\dot{\nu}(t)
is equal to
Clerk and Utami 2007

\frac{d}{dt}\nu(t)\!=i\chi\left[\Sigma_{qq}(t)+\Sigma_{pp}(t)+\mu_{q}^{2}(t)+\mu_{p}^{2}(t)-1\right],

(6)

where
\Sigma_{kk}(t)
and
\mu_{k}(t)
are the time-dependent covariances and means of
W_{eg}(q,p;t)
.

The above expressions highlight how the dephasing may be obtained directly from the moments of
W_{eg}(q,p;t)
. The goal is then to systematically derive equations of motion for these moments, and hence,
\dot{\nu}(t)
in a broad class of meters. In the next section we will move beyond single-mode dispersive readout, and provide a way to accomplish this for systems where the meter is a readout network comprised of an arbitrary number of linear modes, additionally incorporating other coupling mechanisms to the qubit.

II.2
Generalization to multimode systems

In this section, the phase-space procedure for calculating the dephasing is not only generalized to accommodate an arbitrary number of linear modes, but also systematized so that the system of dynamical equations may be immediately constructed from
\mathcal{L}
. We note that bold characters are used in this work to represent arrays, with upper-case letters for matrices and lower-case letters for vectors. Additionally, explicit time dependence is omitted from here on.

We again choose to use the Wigner phase space, and will require that the QPD be a Gaussian, and hence representable entirely by its first two moments. From this ansatz, the Wigner QPD for
\hat{\rho}_{eg}
is

\displaystyle W_{eg}(\bm{r})\,

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{(2\pi)^{N}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2N}}\hskip-2.84526pt{\mathrm{Tr}}_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{R}$}}}\!\left[\hat{\rho}_{eg}e^{i\bm{\xi}^{T}(\hat{\bm{r}}-\bm{r})}\right]\!d\bm{\xi}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{(2\pi)^{N}\sqrt{\det[\bm{\Sigma}]}}e^{-\frac{1}{2}(\bm{r}-\bm{\mu})^{T}\bm{\Sigma}^{-1}(\bm{r}-\bm{\mu})-\nu}

(7)

where
N
is the number of linear modes,
\bm{\Sigma}=\bm{\Sigma}^{T}
is the covariance matrix, and the elements of
\bm{\mu}
are the means. These first and second moments are complex-valued since
\hat{\rho}_{eg}
is in general not Hermitian. As in Eq. (
4
),
\nu
is defined such that
{\mathrm{Tr}}_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{R}$}}}[\hat{\rho}_{eg}]=\exp[-\nu]
.

The above Wigner QPD has been written in an arbitrary quadrature basis,
\bm{r}=(r_{1},\ldots,r_{2N})
, where
r_{k}
is the phase-space coordinate associated with the quadrature operator
\hat{r}_{k}
. The quadrature operators obey the usual canonical commutation relation,
[{\hat{r}_{j}},{\hat{r}_{k}}]=i\Omega_{jk}
, where
\Omega_{jk}
are the elements of the skew-symmetric symplectic form,
\bm{\Omega}=-\bm{\Omega}^{T}
. The choice of basis
\hat{\bm{r}}
determines the structure of
\bm{\Omega}
, with the two most common being

\displaystyle\hat{\bm{r}}=\begin{pmatrix}\hat{q}_{1},\hat{p}_{1},\ldots,\hat{q}_{N},\hat{p}_{N}\end{pmatrix},\,\,\,

\displaystyle\,\,\bm{\Omega}=\bm{I}_{N}\otimes\begin{pmatrix}0&1\\
-1&0\end{pmatrix},

(8)

\displaystyle\hat{\bm{r}}=\begin{pmatrix}\hat{q}_{1},\ldots,\hat{q}_{N},\hat{p}_{1},\ldots,\hat{p}_{N}\end{pmatrix},\,\,\,

\displaystyle\,\,\bm{\Omega}=\begin{pmatrix}0&1\\
-1&0\end{pmatrix}\otimes\bm{I}_{N},

(9)

where
\bm{I}_{N}
is the
N\times N
identity matrix. The operators
\hat{q}_{j}=(\hat{\jmath}^{\dagger}+\hat{\jmath})/\sqrt{2}
and
\hat{p}_{k}=i(\hat{k}^{\dagger}-\hat{k})/\sqrt{2}
, are the usual position and momentum quadratures, respectively, which satisfy
[{\hat{q}_{j}},{\hat{p}_{k}}]=i\delta_{jk}
, and where the creation and annihilation operators obey
[{\hat{\jmath}},{\hat{k}^{\dagger}}]=\delta_{jk}
. By avoiding a specific choice for
\hat{\bm{r}}
, the expressions in this section can be kept as simple and generic as possible.

Having specified what form
\hat{\rho}_{eg}
must take, we can now proceed to describe the restrictions which must be placed on the superoperator
\mathcal{L}
to ensure that
\hat{\rho}_{eg}
is always Gaussian. In short,
\mathcal{L}
must satisfy two simplified conditions. The first of these requires that all linear modes must evolve under linear dynamics. As a consequence, the Hamiltonian acting on the linear mode Hilbert space must be at most a bilinear function of the quadrature operators, while only collapse operators which are linear combinations of quadrature operators are permitted.

The second condition demands that the qubit dynamics cannot couple
\hat{\rho}_{eg}
to any other component of
\hat{\rho}
, so that the dynamical equation for
\hat{\rho}_{eg}
may be extracted from the following:

\frac{d}{dt}\left(\hat{\rho}_{eg}\ket{e}\!\bra{g}\right)=\mathcal{L}\!\left(\hat{\rho}_{eg}\ket{e}\!\bra{g}\right).

(10)

As a consequence, Jaynes-Cummings and Rabi interactions outside the dispersive regime cannot be handled by this method due to the presence of coherent qubit-state transitions. This condition is stricter for systems with multiple qubits, in which case
\hat{\sigma}_{z}
must be a QND-observable of the measurement,
\big\langle\mathcal{L}^{\dagger}(\hat{\sigma}_{z})\big\rangle=0
.

In order to more easily apply these conditions to
\mathcal{L}
, we first express it as

\mathcal{L}(\hat{\rho})=\big(\mathcal{L}_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{R}$}}}+\mathcal{L}_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{Q}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{Q}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{Q}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{Q}$}}\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{R}$}}}+\mathcal{L}_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{Q}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{Q}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{Q}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{Q}$}}}\big)(\hat{\rho}),

(11)

where
\mathcal{L}_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{R}$}}}
contains processes acting solely on the linear modes, those in
\mathcal{L}_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{Q}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{Q}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{Q}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{Q}$}}}
act on the qubit, and
\mathcal{L}_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{Q}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{Q}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{Q}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{Q}$}}\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{R}$}}}
on the combined system. We can now write general forms for the individual components of
\mathcal{L}
, assuming that this represents a Markovian Lindblad master equation. We start with the Lindbladian
\mathcal{L}_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{R}$}}}
, which may written in the quadrature basis as:

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{R}$}}}(\hat{\rho})=-i[{\hat{H}_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{R}$}}}},{\hat{\rho}}]+\!\!\sum_{j,k=1}^{2N}\!\!\Gamma_{jk}\!\left(\!\hat{r}_{k}\hat{\rho}\hat{r}_{j}-\frac{1}{2}\{{\hat{r}_{j}\hat{r}_{k}},{\hat{\rho}}\}\!\right)\!,

(12)

\displaystyle\text{where}\quad\hat{H}_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{R}$}}}=\frac{1}{2}\hat{\bm{r}}^{T}\bm{H}_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{R}$}}}\hat{\bm{r}}+\hat{\bm{r}}^{T}\bm{h}_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{R}$}}}.

Here,
\Gamma_{jk}
are the elements of the matrix
\bm{\Gamma}\in\mathbb{C}^{2N\times 2N}
, the eigenvalues of which are real with dimension of inverse time and so correspond to the decay rates of the system, while the eigenvectors combine the elements
\hat{\bm{r}}
to yield the jump operators. Since the decay rates must be real and non-negative,
\bm{\Gamma}
must be a positive semidefinite Hermitian matrix,
\bm{\Gamma}^{\dagger}=\bm{\Gamma}\geq 0
. The matrix
\bm{H}_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{R}$}}}
contains the coefficients of the bilinear terms from the Hamiltonian
\hat{H}_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{R}$}}}
, while the vector
\bm{h}_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{R}$}}}
corresponds to the linear coefficients.
\bm{H}_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{R}$}}}
therefore contains information about any detunings, squeezing, and beam splitter interactions, whereas the linear term
\bm{h}_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{R}$}}}
represents drive terms of the form
(\varepsilon\hat{a}^{\dagger}+\varepsilon^{*}\hat{a})
. In order for
\hat{H}_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{R}$}}}
to be Hermitian, it is required that
(\bm{H}_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{R}$}}})^{T}=\bm{H}_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{R}$}}}\in\mathbb{R}^{2N\times 2N}
and
\bm{h}_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{R}$}}}\in\mathbb{R}^{2N}
.

For the Lindbladian
\mathcal{L}_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{Q}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{Q}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{Q}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{Q}$}}\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{R}$}}}
, this method is able to accommodate certain dressed-dissipation terms which may appear in the dispersive regime
Boissonneault et al. 2009
. Despite this, only coherent couplings between the qubit and linear modes are considered here for simplicity:

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{Q}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{Q}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{Q}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{Q}$}}\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{R}$}}}(\hat{\rho})=-i[{\hat{\sigma}_{z}\hat{H}_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{Q}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{Q}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{Q}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{Q}$}}\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{R}$}}}},{\hat{\rho}}],

(13)

\displaystyle\text{where}\quad\hat{H}_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{Q}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{Q}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{Q}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{Q}$}}\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{R}$}}}=\frac{1}{2}\hat{\bm{r}}^{T}\bm{H}_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{Q}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{Q}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{Q}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{Q}$}}\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{R}$}}}\hat{\bm{r}}+\hat{\bm{r}}^{T}\bm{h}_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{Q}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{Q}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{Q}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{Q}$}}\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{R}$}}}+h_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{Q}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{Q}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{Q}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{Q}$}}\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{R}$}}}.

As above,
\bm{H}_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{Q}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{Q}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{Q}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{Q}$}}\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{R}$}}}
and
\bm{h}_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{Q}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{Q}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{Q}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{Q}$}}\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{R}$}}}
correspond to the coefficients of the bilinear and linear terms from the Hamiltonian
\hat{H}_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{Q}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{Q}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{Q}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{Q}$}}\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{R}$}}}
, respectively. A constant term
h_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{Q}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{Q}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{Q}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{Q}$}}\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{R}$}}}
, equivalent to an energy shift in the Hamiltonian, is also included, though is only of relevance when calculating the induced qubit frequency shift. Again, to ensure that
\hat{H}_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{Q}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{Q}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{Q}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{Q}$}}\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{R}$}}}
is Hermitian, it is necessary that
(\bm{H}_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{Q}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{Q}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{Q}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{Q}$}}\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{R}$}}})^{T}=\bm{H}_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{Q}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{Q}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{Q}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{Q}$}}\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{R}$}}}\in\mathbb{R}^{2N\times 2N}
,
\bm{h}_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{Q}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{Q}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{Q}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{Q}$}}\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{R}$}}}\in\mathbb{R}^{2N}
, and
h_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{Q}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{Q}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{Q}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{Q}$}}\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{R}$}}}\in\mathbb{R}
. Written in this form, dispersive interactions,
\hat{\sigma}_{z}\hat{a}^{\dagger}\hat{a}
, are contained within
\bm{H}_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{Q}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{Q}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{Q}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{Q}$}}\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{R}$}}}
, while
\bm{h}_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{Q}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{Q}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{Q}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{Q}$}}\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{R}$}}}
corresponds to longitudinal interactions, such as
\hat{\sigma}_{z}(\hat{a}+\hat{a}^{\dagger})
. As a result,
\mathcal{L}_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{Q}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{Q}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{Q}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{Q}$}}\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{R}$}}}
encompasses all possible dispersive and longitudinal measurement processes.

Lastly, the Lindbladian
\mathcal{L}_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{Q}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{Q}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{Q}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{Q}$}}}
, which acts solely on the qubit, takes the following form,

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{Q}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{Q}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{Q}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{Q}$}}}(\hat{\rho})=

\displaystyle-i\frac{\tilde{\omega}_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{Q}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{Q}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{Q}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{Q}$}}}}{2}[{\hat{\sigma}_{z}},{\hat{\rho}}]+\frac{\Gamma_{\varphi}}{2}\mathcal{D}[\hat{\sigma}_{z}](\hat{\rho})

\displaystyle+\Gamma_{-}\mathcal{D}[\hat{\sigma}_{-}](\hat{\rho})+\Gamma_{+}\mathcal{D}[\hat{\sigma}_{+}](\hat{\rho}).

(14)

Upon tracing over the linear modes, the Lindbladian
\mathcal{L}
reduces to the qubit master equation from Eq. (
II.1
), as desired. As in the single mode case, the contribution from the intrinsic qubit processes in
\mathcal{L}_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{Q}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{Q}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{Q}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{Q}$}}}
to the dynamics of
\hat{\rho}_{eg}
may be eliminated by applying a shift,
\hat{\rho}_{eg}\rightarrow e^{-t\left(i\tilde{\omega}_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{Q}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{Q}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{Q}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{Q}$}}}+\Gamma_{2}\right)}\hat{\rho}_{eg}
, and so this contribution to Eq. (
11
) will be omitted.

Using Eq. (
10
), we can now write a dynamical equation for
\hat{\rho}_{eg}
, which is not in Lindblad form:

\frac{d}{dt}\hat{\rho}_{eg}=\mathcal{L}_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{R}$}}}(\hat{\rho}_{eg})-i\{{\hat{H}_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{Q}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{Q}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{Q}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{Q}$}}\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{R}$}}}},{\hat{\rho}_{eg}}\}.

(15)

From this equation, we can now generate differential equations for the moments along with
\nu(t)
, which according to Eq. (
5
), will contain all information about the induced dephasing and frequency shift on the qubit. This is accomplished by converting Eq. (
15
) into a PDE for
W_{eg}(\bm{r})
, which is further transformed into another PDE for the characteristic function of
W_{eg}(\bm{r})
,
w_{eg}(\bm{\xi})={\mathrm{Tr}}_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{R}$}}}[\hat{\rho}_{eg}\exp[i\bm{\xi}^{T}\hat{\bm{r}}]]
, by performing a Fourier transform over all phase-space coordinates. Based on our ansatz for the Wigner function in Eq. (
II.2
), this characteristic function may be written as

w_{eg}(\bm{\xi})=e^{-\frac{1}{2}\bm{\xi}^{T}\bm{\Sigma}\bm{\xi}+i\bm{\mu}^{T}\bm{\xi}-\nu},

(16)

where
\bm{\xi}=(\xi_{1},\ldots,\xi_{2N})
are the coordinates of the reciprocal Fourier space. The characteristic function is more natural to work with since the moments of the QPD may be generated through differentiation about the origin,
\bm{\xi}=\bm{0}
. The PDE for
w_{eg}(\bm{\xi})
is therefore converted into the following system of differential equations for
\bm{\Sigma}
,
\bm{\mu}
, and
\nu
:

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}\bm{\Sigma}

\displaystyle=\bm{A}\bm{\Sigma}+\bm{\Sigma}\bm{A}^{T}-\bm{\Sigma}\bm{B}\bm{\Sigma}+\bm{C},

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}\bm{\mu}

\displaystyle=(\bm{A}-\bm{\Sigma}\bm{B})\bm{\mu}+\bm{d}-\bm{\Sigma}\bm{f},

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}\nu

\displaystyle=g+\bm{f}^{T}\bm{\mu}+\frac{1}{2}\bm{\mu}^{T}\bm{B}\bm{\mu}+\frac{1}{2}{\mathrm{Tr}}\left[\bm{B}\bm{\Sigma}\right],

(17)

where we use the Lindbladians in Eqs. (
12
) and (
13
) to define:

\displaystyle\bm{A}

\displaystyle=\bm{\Omega}\!\left(\bm{H}_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{R}$}}}+\mathrm{Im}[\bm{\Gamma}]\right),

\displaystyle\bm{d}

\displaystyle=\bm{\Omega}\bm{h}_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{R}$}}},

\displaystyle\bm{B}

\displaystyle=2i\bm{H}_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{Q}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{Q}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{Q}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{Q}$}}\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{R}$}}},

\displaystyle\bm{f}

\displaystyle=2i\bm{h}_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{Q}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{Q}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{Q}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{Q}$}}\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{R}$}}},

\displaystyle\bm{C}

\displaystyle=-\bm{\Omega}\!\left(\frac{i}{2}\bm{H}_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{Q}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{Q}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{Q}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{Q}$}}\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{R}$}}}+\real[\bm{\Gamma}]\right)\!\bm{\Omega},

\displaystyle g

\displaystyle=2ih_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{Q}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{Q}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{Q}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{Q}$}}\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{R}$}}}.

(18)

Supporting details for this derivation are given in Appendix
A
. With this result, we may now write explicit solutions for the dephasing in terms of the moments of
W_{eg}(\bm{r})
and the coupling between the qubit and linear modes. Using Eq. (
5
) and the above expressions, we find that
\Gamma_{\mathrm{d}}
may be decomposed into a sum of two components,

\Gamma_{\mathrm{d}}=\Gamma_{\mathrm{d,p}}+\Gamma_{\mathrm{d,m}},

(19)

which may be expressed as

\displaystyle\Gamma_{\mathrm{d,p}}=-{\mathrm{Tr}}\!\left[\mathrm{Im}[\bm{\Sigma}]\bm{H}_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{Q}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{Q}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{Q}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{Q}$}}\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{R}$}}}\right],

(20)

\displaystyle\Gamma_{\mathrm{d,m}}=-2\Big(\mathrm{Im}[\bm{\mu}]^{T}\bm{h}_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{Q}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{Q}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{Q}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{Q}$}}\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{R}$}}}+\mathrm{Im}[\bm{\mu}]^{T}\bm{H}_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{Q}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{Q}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{Q}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{Q}$}}\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{R}$}}}\real[\bm{\mu}]\Big).

(21)

The first component,
\Gamma_{\mathrm{d,p}}
, is termed the
parasitic dephasing
herein, and represents backaction which occurs when the variance of the linear modes coupled to the qubit are above vacuum. A non-zero
\Gamma_{\mathrm{d,p}}
may therefore arise due to thermal fluctuations
Clerk and Utami 2007
or the presence of amplification
Eddins et al. 2019
. It is termed parasitic since it is present even in the absence of any displacement during a dispersive or longitudinal qubit measurement.

The second component,
\Gamma_{\mathrm{d,m}}
, is called the
measurement-induced dephasing
, since it is only non-zero when the linear modes are displaced during a measurement. While the parasitic dephasing is independent of the measurement-induced dephasing, the reverse is not true, since the evolution of
\bm{\mu}
is dependent on the second-order moments in
\bm{\Sigma}
.

Provided that
\hat{\rho}
corresponds to a pure state, the dynamics of
\hat{\rho}_{eg}
, and hence the induced dephasing
Gambetta et al. 2008
, can be extracted directly from the moments of the pointer states
Zurek 1981
,
\hat{\rho}_{ee}
and
\hat{\rho}_{gg}
, which are true quantum states. If
\hat{\rho}
is not pure, which occurs when damped harmonic modes are subject to squeezing or thermal fluctuations
Isar et al. 1999
, then it is not possible to establish a direct connection between the moments of
\hat{\rho}_{eg}
and those of the pointer states. The method presented here for calculating
\hat{\rho}_{eg}
is therefore necessary to model the backaction from integrated qubit-amplifier systems.

II.3
Measurement rate and efficiency

With the induced dephasing defined, the fraction of the qubit information that may be acquired is now characterized using the measurement efficiency

\eta\vcentcolon=\frac{\Gamma_{\mathrm{meas}}}{\Gamma_{\mathrm{d}}}.

(22)

We note that this definition includes inefficiencies due to qubit backaction, similar to Ref.
Eddins et al. 2019
. In this definition,
\Gamma_{\mathrm{meas}}
is the measurement rate, and corresponds to the steady-state rate of change of the amplitude signal-to-noise ratio (SNR) squared:

\displaystyle\Gamma_{\mathrm{meas}}\vcentcolon=

\displaystyle\lim_{t\rightarrow\infty}\frac{\mathrm{SNR}^{2}(t)}{t}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\left\lvert{\langle\hat{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle M$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle M$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle M$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle M$}}}[\omega_{d}]\rangle_{e}-\langle\hat{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle M$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle M$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle M$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle M$}}}[\omega_{d}]\rangle_{g}}\right\rvert^{2}\!\!/4}{\bar{S}_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle M$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle M$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle M$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle M$}}\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle M$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle M$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle M$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle M$}},e}[\omega_{d}]+\bar{S}_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle M$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle M$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle M$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle M$}}\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle M$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle M$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle M$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle M$}},g}[\omega_{d}]+2\bar{n}_{\mathrm{rest}}},

(23)

where
\hat{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle M$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle M$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle M$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle M$}}}
is the measurement operator, which is a function of all monitored quadratures.
\langle\hat{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle M$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle M$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle M$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle M$}}}[\omega]\rangle_{\sigma}
and
\bar{S}_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle M$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle M$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle M$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle M$}}\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle M$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle M$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle M$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle M$}},\sigma}[\omega]
are the measurement operator expectation value and noise power at the output of the readout network, respectively. Both quantities are conditioned on the state of the qubit,
\sigma=e,g
, and are evaluated at the frequency
\omega=\omega_{d}
in the rotating frame. Added noise from downstream components in the readout chain is assumed to be uncorrelated with noise from the readout network, and is parameterized by the independent
\bar{n}_{\mathrm{rest}}
.

While
\Gamma_{\mathrm{meas}}
is dependent on the choice of a specific
\hat{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle M$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle M$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle M$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle M$}}}
, in practice, it is chosen to maximize the difference in displacement between the pointer states, equivalent to the signal component of
\Gamma_{\mathrm{meas}}

Bultink et al. 2018
. The monitored quadratures comprising
\hat{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle M$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle M$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle M$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle M$}}}
are modeled as output fields in the frequency domain, which may be calculated by solving the linear Fourier-transformed Heisenberg-Langevin equations and then applying quantum input-output theory
Gardiner and Collett 1985
. The result is a scattering equation which relates the output and input fields,

\bm{r}_{\mathrm{out}}[\omega]=\mathbf{S}[\omega]\bm{r}_{\mathrm{in}}[\omega]+\mathbf{T}[\omega].

(24)

\bm{r}_{\mathrm{in}}[\omega]
and
\bm{r}_{\mathrm{out}}[\omega]
are the vectors of quadratures for the input and output fields at the reference plane of the readout network, respectively. The readout network is now treated as a scattering center for these propagating input fields, represented by the scattering matrix
\mathbf{S}[\omega]
. The displacement of the output fields by sources other than the input drives, such as longitudinal couplings between the intracavity modes and the qubit, are represented by the matrix
\mathbf{T}[\omega]
. Further details are provided in Appendix
A
.

Assuming the noise may be modeled as a stationary stochastic process, the output-noise power
\bar{S}_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle M$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle M$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle M$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle M$}}\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle M$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle M$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle M$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle M$}},\sigma}[\omega]
is equivalent to the Fourier transform of the autocorrelation function of
\hat{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle M$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle M$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle M$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle M$}}}(t)
by the Wiener–Khinchin theorem
Kubo et al. 1991
. In the case of white noise, the output noise power matrix
\bar{\bm{S}}_{\mathrm{out}}[\omega]
may then be calculated from the input noise power using the scattering matrix,

\bar{\bm{S}}_{\mathrm{out}}[\omega]=\frac{1}{2}\Big(\mathbf{S}[\omega]\bar{\bm{S}}_{\mathrm{in}}\mathbf{S}^{T}[-\omega]+\mathbf{S}[-\omega]\bar{\bm{S}}_{\mathrm{in}}\mathbf{S}^{T}[\omega]\Big),

(25)

where
\bar{\bm{S}}_{\mathrm{in}}
is the covariance matrix of the input fields.

Since the rate of information extracted from the qubit state cannot exceed the resulting backaction
Clerk et al. 2010
;
Korotkov 1999
, the measurement efficiency is limited to the range
0\leq\eta\leq 1
, and hence the measurement rate is necessarily bounded from above by the backaction from the readout network according to Eq. (
22
),
\Gamma_{\mathrm{meas}}\leq\Gamma_{\mathrm{d}}
. Fundamentally, backaction from the measurement drive only gives rise to
\Gamma_{\mathrm{d,m}}
, and so it alone sets the upper limit for the measurement rate,
\Gamma_{\mathrm{meas}}\leq\Gamma_{\mathrm{d,m}}
. The following two requirements should therefore be met in order to maximize
\eta
:

1.

The parasitic dephasing rate
\Gamma_{\mathrm{d,p}}
must be minimized compared to the measurement-induced dephasing rate
\Gamma_{\mathrm{d,m}}
, so that
\Gamma_{\mathrm{d}}
is dominated by the contribution from
\Gamma_{\mathrm{d,m}}
.

2.

The measurement rate must be as close as possible to the measurement-induced dephasing rate,
\Gamma_{\mathrm{meas}}/\Gamma_{\mathrm{d,m}}\rightarrow 1
.

Careful engineering of the readout network is required in order to realize both conditions for a high measurement efficiency, which will be discussed in the next section.

III
Building a readout network

In this section, we consider a concrete implementation of a readout network for efficient qubit measurement, leveraging the tools and lessons learned from Sec.
II
. At its core, this readout network must ideally combine gain to overwhelm downstream system noise and nonreciprocity to protect the qubit from spurious backaction. To achieve nonreciprocity in such a network, interference between at least three parametrically coupled modes must be exploited
Ranzani and Aumentado 2015
;
Metelmann and Clerk 2015
. To retain high efficiency and low backaction, these modes must have carefully-engineered couplings to the qubit and to the external environment. Lastly, to leverage the theory presented in Sec.
II
, the modes must have vanishing self-Kerr and only interact with other modes in the network via bilinear interactions. Below, we introduce such a network and elaborate on these constraints in more detail.

III.1
Three-mode, phase-sensitive readout network

Figure 2:
A three-mode readout network for efficient qubit measurement
. The amplifier (A), buffer (B), and cavity (C) modes form an interferometer to directionally route and amplify readout signals containing information about the qubit (Q). The arrows with color gradients represent beam-splitter interactions between modes J and K with strength
g_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{J}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{J}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{J}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{J}$}}\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{K}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{K}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{K}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{K}$}}}
. The AC beam splitter is arbitrarily chosen to be the only beam splitter with nonzero phase, entirely constraining the interferometer phase
\phi
. The dashed arrow represents dispersive coupling of strength
\chi_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{C}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{C}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{C}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{C}$}}}
, and all other dispersive shifts are neglected in this diagram. The solid gray single-sided arrow represents single mode squeezing at the A mode with strength
\lambda
and phase
\theta
. The oscillating, double-sided arrow represents the input/output coupling to mode B, facilitating a drive of amplitude
\varepsilon
and phase
\varphi
.

We choose a minimal readout network that satisfies the constraints listed above, inspired by Refs.
Lecocq et al. 2020
;
Lecocq et al. 2021
and shown in Fig.
2
. The network consists of three linear modes: the
cavity
(C) mode that couples dispersively to the qubit, the
amplifier
(A) mode wherein the qubit measurement signal is phase-sensitively amplified, and the
buffer
(B) mode which acts as the input/output mode. These three modes are coupled via parametric frequency conversion to form an interferometer (IFO) that nonreciprocally routes readout signals and noise within the network. Only dispersive interactions between the qubit and the individual modes in the readout network are included, and any corrections to the dispersive terms are minimal and therefore omitted. The resulting interaction Hamiltonian in the rotating frame is then:

\hat{H}=\underbrace{\hat{H}_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{IFO}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{IFO}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{IFO}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{IFO}$}}}+\hat{H}_{\mathrm{gain}}+\hat{H}_{\mathrm{drive}}}_{\textstyle\hat{H}_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{R}$}}}}+\hat{\sigma}_{z}\hat{H}_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{Q}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{Q}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{Q}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{Q}$}}\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{R}$}}},

(26)

where

\displaystyle\hat{H}_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{IFO}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{IFO}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{IFO}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{IFO}$}}}=\,

\displaystyle g_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{A}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{A}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{A}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{A}$}}\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{B}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{B}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{B}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{B}$}}}\hat{a}^{\dagger}\hat{b}+g_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{B}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{B}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{B}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{B}$}}\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{C}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{C}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{C}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{C}$}}}\hat{b}^{\dagger}\hat{c}+g_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{A}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{A}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{A}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{A}$}}\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{C}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{C}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{C}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{C}$}}}e^{i\phi}\hat{a}^{\dagger}\hat{c}+\mathrm{h.c.},

(27)

\displaystyle\hat{H}_{\mathrm{gain}}=\,

\displaystyle\frac{\lambda}{2}\left(e^{i\theta}\hat{a}^{\dagger\,2}+e^{-i\theta}\hat{a}^{2}\right),

(28)

\displaystyle\hat{H}_{\mathrm{drive}}=\,

\displaystyle\varepsilon\left(e^{i\varphi}\hat{b}+e^{-i\varphi}\hat{b}^{\dagger}\right),

(29)

\displaystyle\hat{H}_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{Q}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{Q}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{Q}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{Q}$}}\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{R}$}}}=

\displaystyle\hskip-5.69054pt\sum_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{K}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{K}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{K}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{K}$}}=\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{A}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{A}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{A}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{A}$}},\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{B}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{B}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{B}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{B}$}},\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{C}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{C}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{C}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{C}$}}}\hskip-5.69054pt\chi_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{K}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{K}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{K}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{K}$}}}\hat{k}^{\dagger}\hat{k}.

(30)

Above, the canonical creation and annihilation operators
\hat{k}^{(\dagger)}
for modes A, B, and C are denoted
\hat{a}^{(\dagger)}
,
\hat{b}^{(\dagger)}
, and
\hat{c}^{(\dagger)}
, respectively.
\phi\vcentcolon=\phi_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{A}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{A}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{A}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{A}$}}\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{B}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{B}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{B}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{B}$}}}+\phi_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{B}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{B}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{B}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{B}$}}\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{C}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{C}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{C}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{C}$}}}-\phi_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{A}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{A}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{A}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{A}$}}\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{C}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{C}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{C}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{C}$}}}
is defined as the
interferometer phase
, and
g_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{J}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{J}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{J}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{J}$}}\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{K}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{K}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{K}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{K}$}}}\in\mathbb{R}
and
\phi_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{J}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{J}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{J}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{J}$}}\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{K}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{K}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{K}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{K}$}}}
are the strength and phase of the pairwise beam-splitter coupling of arbitrary modes
\mathrm{J},\mathrm{K}\in\{\mathrm{A},\mathrm{B},\mathrm{C}\}
,
\mathrm{J}\neq\mathrm{K}
.
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}
and
\theta
define the strength and phase of the single-mode squeezing interaction at mode A. Optimally, the quadrature containing qubit information will be amplified to increase both
\langle\hat{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle M$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle M$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle M$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle M$}}}\rangle_{\sigma}
and
\bar{S}_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle M$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle M$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle M$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle M$}}\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle M$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle M$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle M$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle M$}},\sigma}
in Eq. (
II.3
), overwhelming the contributions of
\bar{n}_{\mathrm{rest}}
and improving the measurement rate. Dispersive couplings between the qubit and all three modes are included, however ideally the dispersive coupling between the qubit and the cavity mode should be the dominant interaction in
\hat{H}_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{Q}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{Q}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{Q}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{Q}$}}\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{R}$}}}
(
\chi_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{C}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{C}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{C}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{C}$}}}\gg\chi_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{B}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{B}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{B}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{B}$}}},\chi_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{A}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{A}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{A}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{A}$}}}
).

These coherent processes compete with incoherent processes in the system, set by the total dissipation rate
\gamma_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{K}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{K}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{K}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{K}$}}}
and thermal occupancy
n^{\mathrm{th}}_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{K}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{K}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{K}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{K}$}}}
of each mode (
\mathrm{K}=\mathrm{A},\mathrm{B},\mathrm{C}
). The resulting general Lindbladian can be written as:

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}(\hat{\rho})=

\displaystyle-i[{\hat{H}_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{R}$}}}+\hat{\sigma}_{z}\hat{H}_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{Q}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{Q}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{Q}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{Q}$}}\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{R}$}}}},{\hat{\rho}}]

\displaystyle+\hskip-8.53581pt\sum_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{K}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{K}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{K}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{K}$}}=\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{A}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{A}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{A}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{A}$}},\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{B}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{B}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{B}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{B}$}},\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{C}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{C}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{C}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{C}$}}}\hskip-7.11317pt\!\gamma_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{K}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{K}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{K}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{K}$}}}\!\left((n^{\mathrm{th}}_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{K}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{K}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{K}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{K}$}}}+1)\mathcal{D}[\hat{k}]+n^{\mathrm{th}}_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{K}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{K}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{K}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{K}$}}}\mathcal{D}[\hat{k}^{\dagger}]\right)\!(\hat{\rho}),

(31)

allowing the construction of the matrix
\bm{\Gamma}
through comparison with Eq. (
12
). Ideally, all thermal occupancies and internal losses are minimized, and only the B mode is strongly coupled to an input/output port with rate
\gamma_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{B}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{B}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{B}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{B}$}}}^{\mathrm{ext}}
such that
\gamma_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{B}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{B}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{B}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{B}$}}}^{\mathrm{ext}}\approx\gamma_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{B}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{B}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{B}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{B}$}}}\gg\gamma_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{A}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{A}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{A}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{A}$}}},\gamma_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{C}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{C}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{C}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{C}$}}}
.
\varepsilon
and
\varphi
define the strength and phase of a resonant drive (
\omega=0
in the rotating frame) to this port, respectively. Additionally, to minimally degrade the measurement rate, signals present in the readout network must preferentially leave the system through the monitored B mode. This requires that all beam-splitter coupling rates also greatly exceed the total linewidths of modes A and C (
g_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{J}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{J}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{J}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{J}$}}\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{K}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{K}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{K}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{K}$}}},\gamma_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{B}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{B}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{B}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{B}$}}}\gg\gamma_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{A}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{A}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{A}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{A}$}}},\gamma_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{C}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{C}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{C}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{C}$}}}
). In this regime, the readout network essentially behaves as a single-port device in which the nonreciprocal behavior is evident primarily in the internal dynamics.

The desired internal dynamics can be understood in the following simplified picture: a signal injected into mode B travels first to mode C to pick up a qubit-state dependent phase shift, then to mode A to acquire phase-sensitive gain, and finally output through mode B. This configuration minimizes parasitic dephasing by suppressing the propagation of amplified noise from mode A to mode C. Achieving this circulation requires ideal interference within the readout network, placing further constraints on the beam-splitter interaction strengths and phases.

These constraints can be found by maximizing the directional scattering asymmetry between modes A and C, quantified by the normalized degree of nonreciprocity
Orr 2025
:

N^{(\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{K}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{K}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{K}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{K}$}},\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{J}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{J}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{J}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{J}$}})}[\omega]\vcentcolon=\frac{||{\mathbf{S}_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{J}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{J}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{J}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{J}$}}\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{K}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{K}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{K}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{K}$}}}[\omega]}||_{F}^{2}-||{\mathbf{S}_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{K}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{K}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{K}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{K}$}}\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{J}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{J}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{J}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{J}$}}}[\omega]}||_{F}^{2}}{||{\mathbf{S}_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{J}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{J}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{J}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{J}$}}\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{K}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{K}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{K}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{K}$}}}[\omega]}||_{F}^{2}+||{\mathbf{S}_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{K}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{K}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{K}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{K}$}}\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{J}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{J}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{J}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{J}$}}}[\omega]}||_{F}^{2}},

(32)

where
||{\cdot}||_{F}
is the Frobenius norm, and
\mathbf{S}_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{J}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{J}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{J}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{J}$}}\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{K}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{K}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{K}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{K}$}}}[\omega]
is a
2\times 2
sub-matrix of the total scattering matrix
\mathbf{S}[\omega]
corresponding to the scattering from the input of some mode K to the output of another mode J. For example,
\mathbf{S}_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{J}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{J}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{J}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{J}$}}\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{K}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{K}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{K}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{K}$}}}[\omega]
may correspond to
(\hat{\jmath}_{\mathrm{out}}\,\,\,\hat{\jmath}^{\dagger}_{\mathrm{out}})^{T}\!=\mathbf{S}_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{J}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{J}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{J}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{J}$}}\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{K}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{K}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{K}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{K}$}}}[\omega](\hat{k}_{\mathrm{in}}\,\,\,\hat{k}^{\dagger}_{\mathrm{in}})^{T}
; however, note that the definition Eq. (
32
) is independent of the choice of basis for
\mathbf{S}[\omega]
.

By construction
N^{(\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{K}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{K}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{K}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{K}$}},\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{J}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{J}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{J}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{J}$}})}[\omega]\in[-1,1]
, taking a null value for reciprocal scattering, a negative value for a dominant
\mathrm{J}\rightarrow\mathrm{K}
scattering, and a positive value for dominant
\mathrm{K}\rightarrow\mathrm{J}
scattering. Setting
\chi_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{A}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{A}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{A}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{A}$}}},\chi_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{B}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{B}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{B}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{B}$}}}=0
for the sake of simplicity, the normalized degree of nonreciprocity between the modes A and C in the readout network at the resonance frequency
N^{(\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{C}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{C}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{C}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{C}$}},\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{A}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{A}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{A}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{A}$}})}=N^{(\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{C}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{C}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{C}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{C}$}},\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{A}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{A}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{A}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{A}$}})}[\omega=0]
, is
