N^{(\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{C}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{C}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{C}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{C}$}},\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{A}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{A}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{A}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{A}$}})}=-2\sin(\phi)\left(\sqrt{\frac{\mathcal{C}_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{A}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{A}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{A}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{A}$}}\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{B}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{B}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{B}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{B}$}}}\mathcal{C}_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{B}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{B}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{B}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{B}$}}\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{C}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{C}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{C}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{C}$}}}}{\mathcal{C}_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{A}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{A}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{A}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{A}$}}\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{C}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{C}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{C}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{C}$}}}}}+\sqrt{\frac{\mathcal{C}_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{A}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{A}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{A}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{A}$}}\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{C}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{C}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{C}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{C}$}}}}{\mathcal{C}_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{A}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{A}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{A}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{A}$}}\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{B}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{B}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{B}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{B}$}}}\mathcal{C}_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{B}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{B}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{B}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{B}$}}\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{C}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{C}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{C}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{C}$}}}}}\right)^{\!\!-1},

(33)

where
\mathcal{C}_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{J}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{J}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{J}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{J}$}}\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{K}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{K}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{K}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{K}$}}}\vcentcolon=4g_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{J}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{J}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{J}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{J}$}}\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{K}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{K}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{K}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{K}$}}}^{2}/\gamma_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{J}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{J}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{J}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{J}$}}}\gamma_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{K}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{K}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{K}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{K}$}}}
is the cooperativity between modes J and K. The desired circulation requires that
N^{(\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{C}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{C}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{C}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{C}$}},\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{A}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{A}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{A}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{A}$}})}=1
, which is met with the following conditions:

\frac{\mathcal{C}_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{A}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{A}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{A}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{A}$}}\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{B}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{B}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{B}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{B}$}}}\mathcal{C}_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{B}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{B}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{B}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{B}$}}\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{C}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{C}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{C}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{C}$}}}}{\mathcal{C}_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{A}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{A}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{A}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{A}$}}\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{C}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{C}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{C}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{C}$}}}}=1,\qquad\phi=-\frac{\pi}{2}.

(34)

The constraint on the cooperativities effectively balances the scattering amplitudes along each arm of the interferometer, and the phase condition establishes maximal interference, with
\mathrm{sgn}(\phi)
determining the prevailing direction of the nonreciprocal scattering. Such conditions can be understood as balancing coherent and dissipative processes to engineer nonreciprocity
Metelmann and Clerk 2015
.
To summarize, this readout network requires three modes with low internal loss and tunable all-to-all parametric interactions. In this network, one mode should be primarily dispersively coupled to the qubit and another mode should be strongly coupled to its environment. Below we introduce an experimental implementation of such a readout network.

III.2
Experimental implementation

Figure 3:
Device overview.

(a)
Sliced COMSOL model of the device. A transmon qubit is housed on a chip to the left, a
\lambda/4
coaxial cavity in the center constitutes the cavity (C) mode, and a secondary chip on the right contains the amplifier (A) and buffer (B) modes. This chip also includes a dc SQUID and its inductively-coupled bias line, as well as a port to drive both the qubit and the B mode.
(b)
Measured and simulated frequencies of each mode and the qubit as a function of the DC flux bias through the SQUID. The black dashed line marks the operating flux bias of 0.246
\Phi_{0}
.
(c)
Table of the mode frequencies, linewidths, and dispersive couplings with the qubit for each mode at the operating flux bias (
\chi_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{Q}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{Q}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{Q}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{Q}$}}}
denotes the self-Kerr or anharmonicity of the transmon).

The three-mode readout network is implemented with a hybrid 2D/3D device operating in a dilution refrigerator at
15~\mathrm{mK}
. As shown in Fig.
3
(a), the device consists of a 3D coaxial
\lambda/4
cavity
Reagor et al. 2016
which houses the cavity (C) mode, as well as two chips. One chip contains a fixed-frequency transmon qubit (Q) dispersively coupled to the C mode. The other chip introduces a lumped resonance to act as the amplifier (A) mode and a distributed resonance to act as the buffer (B) mode, both of which have comparatively weak dispersive interactions with the qubit. Importantly, this latter chip includes a dc SQUID which participates in all three linear modes, enabling frequency tunability and parametric interactions. Lastly, the chip includes a fast-flux line to bias the dc SQUID and a port to drive both the B mode and the qubit. More details regarding the device design and EM simulations are included in Appendix
G
.

In Fig.
3
(b), we show the measured and simulated mode frequencies as a function of flux bias. As desired, the linear mode frequencies tune as a function of flux bias, and the transmon frequency remains approximately constant, retaining low sensitivity to flux noise. The linewidths of the linear modes and achievable parametric interaction strengths are also flux-dependent. We therefore choose a bias point that maximizes
\gamma_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{B}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{B}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{B}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{B}$}}}
while both minimizing internal losses (
\gamma_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{A}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{A}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{A}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{A}$}}}
,
\gamma_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{C}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{C}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{C}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{C}$}}}
) and maintaining appreciable parametric interaction strengths. In the following, the flux bias is fixed to
0.246~\Phi_{0}
and the extracted frequencies, linewidths and dispersive shifts at this operating point are tabulated in Fig.
3
(c). Further details regarding the device characterization are available in Appendix
D
.

From the extracted device parameters, it is evident that
\gamma_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{B}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{B}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{B}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{B}$}}}\gg\gamma_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{A}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{A}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{A}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{A}$}}},\gamma_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{C}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{C}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{C}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{C}$}}}
and
\chi_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{C}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{C}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{C}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{C}$}}}\gg\chi_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{B}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{B}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{B}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{B}$}}},\chi_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{A}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{A}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{A}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{A}$}}}
, matching the requirements laid out in Sec.
III.1
. Following this basic characterization, we will now leverage the theory developed in Sec.
II
to study the performance of this readout network and its effect on the qubit coherence.

Figure 4:
Experimental sequences to quantify qubit measurement.

(a)

\Gamma_{\mathrm{d}}
is extracted with interleaved
T_{1}
(blue dashed box) and
T_{2}^{\mathrm{echo}}
(purple dashed box) measurements in the presence of arbitrary linear operations on the modes, denoted
U
. In reality, the operations in
U
begin before the qubit state preparation to ensure the multimode system is in the steady state. The B mode rail is drawn thicker to represent the mode’s comparatively large linewidth. Example
T_{1}
and
T_{2}^{\mathrm{echo}}
traces in the absence of any readout network drives or parametric interactions (
U=\bm{I}_{3}
) are shown below the sequence diagram. (b) To extract
\Gamma_{\mathrm{meas}}
, the qubit is prepared in either the ground or excited state, a set of operations
U
that contains a weak B mode drive is initiated, then the steady state signal is averaged over time
\tau
. Single shot histograms (plotted for the case of the interferometer with
\phi=0.55\pi
and
\tau
= 700 ns) are fit to extract
\mathrm{SNR}^{2}
as a function of
\tau
, producing a linear relationship with slope
\Gamma_{\mathrm{meas}}
.

IV
Experimental qubit measurement and dephasing

In this section, we experimentally measure the rates
\Gamma_{\mathrm{meas}}
and
\Gamma_{\mathrm{d}}
in various readout network configurations, comparing them to theoretical predictions calculated with the methodology presented in Sec.
II
. Experimentally, the dephasing rate is extracted by interleaving relaxation (
T_{1}
) and Hahn echo coherence (
T_{2}^{\mathrm{echo}}
) measurements in the presence of steady-state readout network parametric interactions and drives
U
, see Fig.
4
(a). At the end of each sequence, the state of the qubit is read out and reset with the protocol detailed in Appendix
E
. The Hahn echo sequence suppresses low-frequency dephasing, and assuming an absence of high-frequency contributions intrinsic to the qubit, dephasing due to the readout network can be approximated as
\Gamma_{\mathrm{d}}\approx(T_{2}^{\mathrm{echo}})^{-1}-(2T_{1})^{-1}
. The measurement rate
\Gamma_{\mathrm{meas}}
is extracted from single-shot qubit measurements for each qubit state
\sigma=e,g
and various signal averaging times
\tau
, as shown in Fig.
4
. The means
\mu_{\sigma}=\langle\hat{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle M$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle M$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle M$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle M$}}}[0]\rangle_{\sigma}
and variances
\Sigma_{\sigma}=(1/\tau)(\bar{S}_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle M$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle M$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle M$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle M$}}\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle M$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle M$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle M$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle M$}},\sigma}[0]+\bar{n}_{\mathrm{rest}})
of the resulting distributions define the
\mathrm{SNR}^{2}
in Eq. (
II.3
), which is linearly fit with respect to
\tau
to extract
\Gamma_{\mathrm{meas}}
.

In the following subsections, we use the qubit dephasing to independently calibrate the thermal occupancy of each mode with a single free parameter at a time. We then operate the readout network as a nonreciprocal three-mode interferometer, demonstrating quantitative understanding of the qubit dephasing and measurement rates.

Figure 5:
Mean thermal occupancy extraction.
Experimental data and theoretical fits (with one free parameter each) of the qubit’s parasitic dephasing rate
\Gamma_{\mathrm{d,p}}
as a function of
(a)
AC beam-splitter strength to extract
n^{\mathrm{th}}_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{A}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{A}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{A}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{A}$}}}=0.33
and
(b)
BC beam-splitter strength to extract
n^{\mathrm{th}}_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{B}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{B}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{B}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{B}$}}}=0.0030
.

IV.1
Thermal occupancy measurements

Thermal fluctuations in the readout network introduce excess qubit dephasing and reduce the qubit measurement rate. To quantify each mode’s contributions to these fluctuations, the parasitic dephasing rate (
\Gamma_{\mathrm{d,p}}
) of the qubit is extracted by measuring the qubit’s dephasing rate (
\Gamma_{\mathrm{d}}
) in some simple readout network configurations without measurement drives, such that
\Gamma_{\mathrm{d}}(\varepsilon=0)=\Gamma_{\mathrm{d,p}}
.

In the absence of any parametric interactions, the readout network approximates a single mode (C) dispersively coupled to the qubit (due to the fact that
\chi_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{C}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{C}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{C}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{C}$}}}\gg\chi_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{A}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{A}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{A}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{A}$}}},\chi_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{B}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{B}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{B}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{B}$}}}
). In this limit, the theory from Sec.
II
reproduces the known result for thermal state-induced dephasing expressed in Ref.
Clerk and Utami 2007
(see Appendix
B.2
). Assuming the magnitude of thermal fluctuations is small, the thermal occupancy of mode C,
n^{\mathrm{th}}_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{C}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{C}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{C}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{C}$}}}
, can be computed with the simple expression:

n^{\mathrm{th}}_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{C}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{C}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{C}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{C}$}}}\approx\frac{\gamma_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{C}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{C}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{C}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{C}$}}}^{2}+4\chi_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{C}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{C}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{C}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{C}$}}}^{2}}{4\gamma_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{C}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{C}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{C}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{C}$}}}\chi_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{C}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{C}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{C}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{C}$}}}^{2}}\Gamma_{\mathrm{d,p}}=0.0081.

(35)

This occupancy is standard for state-of-the-art shielding, filtering, and thermalization of the cavity and its control lines.

By design, the qubit is significantly less sensitive to thermal fluctuations in modes A and B, owing to the weak dispersive interaction between each of these modes and the qubit. To enhance the qubit’s sensitivity to these fluctuations, a single beam-splitter interaction between mode
\mathrm{K}=\mathrm{A},\mathrm{B}
and mode C is introduced:

\hat{H}_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{R}$}}}=g_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{K}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{K}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{K}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{K}$}}\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{C}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{C}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{C}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{C}$}}}\left(\hat{k}^{\dagger}\hat{c}+\hat{k}\hat{c}^{\dagger}\right),

(36)

where
g_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{K}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{K}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{K}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{K}$}}\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{C}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{C}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{C}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{C}$}}}
is the beam-splitter interaction strength. Conceptually, this beam-splitter interaction allows the participating modes to share their thermal fluctuations, dissipation rates, and dispersive shifts. As a consequence, the qubit’s parasitic dephasing rate will nontrivially depend on
g_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{K}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{K}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{K}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{K}$}}\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{C}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{C}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{C}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{C}$}}}
. Figs.
5
(a) and (b) show the measured
\Gamma_{\mathrm{d,p}}
with respect to the coupling strengths
g_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{A}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{A}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{A}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{A}$}}\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{C}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{C}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{C}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{C}$}}}
and
g_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{B}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{B}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{B}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{B}$}}\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{C}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{C}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{C}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{C}$}}}
, independently calibrated in Appendix
D
. Numerical fits of Eq. (
20
) to the data yield the mode occupancies
n^{\mathrm{th}}_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{A}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{A}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{A}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{A}$}}}=0.33
and
n^{\mathrm{th}}_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{B}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{B}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{B}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{B}$}}}=0.0030
. We attribute the larger thermal occupancy of mode A to its stronger coupling to the flux line, which is less filtered and attenuated than the signal line.

These studies of thermal-induced dephasing have facilitated the independent extraction of each mode’s thermal occupancy, as well as the initial validation of the theoretical framework in Sec.
II
with data. Building on this agreement, we now study the readout network in a more complex mode of operation.

IV.2
Interferometer

We now demonstrate the nonreciprocal routing of noise and signals within the readout network by forming an interferometer using three beam-splitter interactions between modes A, B, and C. This results in the Hamiltonian
\hat{H}_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{R}$}}}=\hat{H}_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{IFO}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{IFO}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{IFO}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{IFO}$}}}+\hat{H}_{\mathrm{drive}}
, with each term being defined in Eq. (
26
). The beam-splitter coupling strengths are experimentally tuned to
(g_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{A}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{A}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{A}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{A}$}}\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{B}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{B}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{B}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{B}$}}},g_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{B}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{B}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{B}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{B}$}}\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{C}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{C}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{C}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{C}$}}},g_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{A}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{A}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{A}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{A}$}}\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{C}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{C}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{C}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{C}$}}})/2\pi=(14.5,7.98,6.2)
MHz, approximating the optimal nonreciprocal scattering condition in Eq. (
34
). The interferometer phase
\phi
is experimentally calibrated up to an overall shift of
\pi
by measuring the scattering parameter in reflection off of the B mode. Further details regarding the device tuneup are included in Appendix
D.8
.

Figure 6:
Qubit measurement and backaction with a multimode interferometer.
Experimental data and theoretical predictions for the
(a)
parasitic dephasing rate,
(b)
total dephasing rate including parasitic and measurement contributions,
(c)
measurement rate, and
(d)
measurement efficiency of the qubit as a function of interferometer phase. The parametric coupling rates in this configuration are
(g_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{A}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{A}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{A}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{A}$}}\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{B}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{B}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{B}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{B}$}}},g_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{B}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{B}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{B}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{B}$}}\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{C}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{C}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{C}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{C}$}}},g_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{A}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{A}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{A}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{A}$}}\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{C}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{C}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{C}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{C}$}}})/2\pi=(14.5,7.98,6.2)
MHz, the drive strength for the relevant quantities is fixed at
\varepsilon\approx 3.7
MHz, and other device parameters are given in Fig.
3
(c) and Appendix
D
.

The nonreciprocal routing of thermal photons within the readout network is observed by measuring the parasitic dephasing rate
\Gamma_{\mathrm{d,p}}
as a function of the interferometer phase. In Fig.
6
(a), the data, in good agreement with the theoretical model, show a clear asymmetry of
\Gamma_{\mathrm{d,p}}
with respect to
\phi
. Due to the large imbalance in thermal occupancies (
n^{\mathrm{th}}_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{A}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{A}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{A}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{A}$}}}\gg n^{\mathrm{th}}_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{B}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{B}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{B}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{B}$}}},n^{\mathrm{th}}_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{C}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{C}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{C}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{C}$}}}
), the parasitic dephasing is greater when thermal photons in A are directed towards C:
\Gamma_{\mathrm{d,p}}(\phi=-\pi/2)<\Gamma_{\mathrm{d,p}}(\phi=+\pi/2)
. This asymmetry allows for the disambiguation of the
\pi
-offset in
\phi
, which is the only fit parameter in the model.

We now add a drive to the B mode, with strength
\varepsilon\approx 3.7
MHz independently calibrated in Appendix
D.6
. This drive introduces a measurement-induced dephasing (
\Gamma_{\mathrm{d,m}}
) contribution to
\Gamma_{\mathrm{d}}
, allowing us to measure the qubit with rate
\Gamma_{\mathrm{meas}}\leq\Gamma_{\mathrm{d,m}}
. The measured total dephasing and measurement rates as a function of interferometer phase are shown in Fig.
6
(b) and (c) respectively. The theory predictions show considerable agreement with the measured data with no free parameters. At this drive strength, and for both circulation directions
\phi=\pm\pi/2
, the measurement-induced dephasing overwhelms the parasitic dephasing,
\Gamma_{\mathrm{d,m}}\gg\Gamma_{\mathrm{d,p}}
. Because
g_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{J}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{J}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{J}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{J}$}}\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{K}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{K}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{K}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{K}$}}},\gamma_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{B}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{B}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{B}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{B}$}}}\gg\gamma_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{A}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{A}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{A}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{A}$}}},\gamma_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{C}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{C}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{C}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{C}$}}}
, measurement signals entering the network will measure the qubit and exit through the B mode regardless of the circulation direction, leading to approximately symmetric measurement and dephasing rates with respect to interferometer phase.

The measurement efficiency
\eta
, defined in Eq. (
22
) as the ratio of
\Gamma_{\mathrm{meas}}
and
\Gamma_{\mathrm{d}}
, is shown in Fig.
6
(d) for this interferometer. The efficiency shows a weak dependence on
\phi
, further highlighting the fact that measurement signals preferentially leave mode B regardless of how they are routed within the network. The average efficiency of
\sim 7\%
is principally determined by the added noise of the subsequent measurement chain diagrammed in Appendix
H
. In Appendix
D.7
, this added noise is independently calibrated as
\bar{n}_{\mathrm{rest}}\approx 5.24
quanta.

Figure 7:
Gaussian Wigner distributions for the three internal modes of the embedded amplifier.
Distributions plotted as 99% confidence ellipses, conditioned on the state of the qubit, either the ground (blue) or excited (red) state, with black points representing the distribution centers. Dashed contours and gray center points correspond to the intracavity states absent any squeezing. The parameters are motivated by the three-mode readout network, modified to exactly satisfy the conditions for nonreciprocity between the A and C modes from Eq. (
34
). Linewidths of
(\gamma_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{A}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{A}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{A}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{A}$}}},\gamma_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{B}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{B}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{B}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{B}$}}},\gamma_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{C}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{C}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{C}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{C}$}}})/2\pi=(0.115,39.0,0.110)
MHz, and beam-splitter rates of
(g_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{A}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{A}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{A}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{A}$}}\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{B}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{B}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{B}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{B}$}}},g_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{B}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{B}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{B}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{B}$}}\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{C}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{C}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{C}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{C}$}}},g_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{A}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{A}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{A}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{A}$}}\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{C}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{C}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{C}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{C}$}}})/2\pi=(14.5,7.98,5.934)
MHz are used. Only mode C is coupled to the qubit,
(\chi_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{A}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{A}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{A}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{A}$}}},\chi_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{B}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{B}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{B}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{B}$}}},\chi_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{C}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{C}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{C}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{C}$}}})/2\pi=(0,0,2.754)
MHz, and all baths are in the vacuum,
n^{\mathrm{th}}_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{K}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{K}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{K}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{K}$}}}=0
. The drive amplitude is
\varepsilon/2\pi=20
MHz, with phase
\varphi=0
. The single-mode squeezing at mode A is tuned to realize a gain of
\mathcal{G}_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{B}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{B}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{B}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{B}$}}\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{B}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{B}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{B}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{B}$}}}=20
dB, equating to
\lambda/2\pi=9.83
MHz with a phase of
\theta=-\pi/2
. It may be noted that the displacement of the amplifier mode C state is identical to that of the interferometer, i.e. without any amplification, while the variance is close to that of a vacuum state.

This downstream added noise motivates the inclusion of gain at mode A, completing the readout network proposed in Sec.
III
. While we were able to observe the desired single-mode squeezing interaction at mode A, it was weak and overshadowed by a spurious gain process of unknown origin, which quickly brought the device into an unstable regime (see Appendix
F
). With the use of nonlinear simulation techniques
Peng et al. 2022
and Kerr-nulling mixing elements
Sivak et al. 2019
, we suspect this process may be identified and avoided in future efforts. In the next section, we therefore utilize the experimentally-vetted theory to demonstrate the promise for high-efficiency qubit readout in future experiments, using a device with similar parameters.

V
Embedded Amplifier Theory

The next step in understanding the full readout network is to add single mode squeezing at mode A of the interferometer discussed in the previous section. This system is now the phase-sensitive, nonreciprocal parametric amplifier modeled by the Hamiltonian in Eq. (
26
). To simplify this theoretical analysis, it is assumed that all baths are in the vacuum state,
n^{\mathrm{th}}_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{K}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{K}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{K}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{K}$}}}\approx 0
, and the dispersive couplings of modes A and B may be neglected,
\chi_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{A}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{A}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{A}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{A}$}}},\chi_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{B}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{B}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{B}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{B}$}}}=0
. The beam-splitter coupling rates are similar to those of the interferometer, only slightly altered to better match the nonreciprocity condition in Eq. (
34
):
(g_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{A}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{A}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{A}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{A}$}}\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{B}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{B}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{B}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{B}$}}},g_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{B}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{B}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{B}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{B}$}}\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{C}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{C}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{C}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{C}$}}},g_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{A}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{A}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{A}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{A}$}}\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{C}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{C}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{C}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{C}$}}})/2\pi=(14.5,7.98,5.934)
MHz.

To keep the amplifier in the stable regime, we limit the squeezing interaction strength according to the critical stability criteria, discussed in Appendix
C.2
. Saturating this condition corresponds to
\lambda_{\mathrm{max}}/2\pi=10.84
MHz, and results in gain in reflection off of mode B that grows without bound (
\mathcal{G}_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{B}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{B}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{B}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{B}$}}\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{B}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{B}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{B}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{B}$}}}\rightarrow\infty
). This upper limit on the squeezing results in the following inequality:

\mathcal{C}_{\lambda}<1+\mathcal{C}_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{A}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{A}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{A}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{A}$}}\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{B}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{B}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{B}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{B}$}}},

(37)

where
\mathcal{C}_{\lambda}\vcentcolon=2\lambda/\gamma_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{A}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{A}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{A}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{A}$}}}
is the squeezing cooperativity. This condition corresponds to the requirement that anti-damping from the squeezing cannot be greater than the enhanced mode A damping rate,
\lambda<\gamma_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{A}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{A}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{A}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{A}$}},\mathrm{eff}}/2
where
\gamma_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{A}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{A}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{A}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{A}$}},\mathrm{eff}}=\gamma_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{A}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{A}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{A}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{A}$}}}(1+\mathcal{C}_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{A}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{A}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{A}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{A}$}}\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{B}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{B}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{B}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{B}$}}})
. In order to amplify the measurement field quadrature containing qubit information, the relative phase between the squeezing and the drive on mode B is set to
\theta-2\varphi=-\pi/2
.

The simulated internal state of the embedded amplifier is shown in Fig.
7
. In the presence of a gain of
\mathcal{G}_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{B}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{B}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{B}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{B}$}}\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{B}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{B}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{B}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{B}$}}}=20
dB, the phase-sensitive amplification of the intracavity states is apparent in the A and B modes. In comparison, the nonreciprocity hampers amplification of the state of mode C. As a result, the variance of mode C only deviates slightly from that of a vacuum state, and the displacement of the intracavity state of mode C is identical to the case with no squeezing (
\lambda/2\pi=0
MHz). The consequence of this observed behavior on the dephasing rates, as well as the qubit measurement rate, will be discussed below, with supporting details provided in Appendix
C
.

V.1
Measurement and dephasing rates

The discussion at the end of Sec.
II.2
provided a heuristic connection between the parasitic and measurement-induced dephasing rates and the variance and displacement of the internal states of any linear modes directly coupled to the qubit. As a consequence, the state of mode C observed in Fig.
7
indicates that the qubit backaction ideally has minimal dependence on amplifier gain.

In fact, provided the drive on mode B and all three beam-splitter interactions have zero detuning, the nonreciprocity of the network renders the measurement-induced dephasing entirely independent of any squeezing and thermal noise from mode A. As a result,
\Gamma_{\mathrm{d,m}}
is identical to the result obtained by turning off both the AB and AC beam splitters, effectively decoupling mode A from the system:

\Gamma_{\mathrm{d,m}}\!\left(N^{(\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{C}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{C}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{C}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{C}$}},\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{A}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{A}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{A}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{A}$}})}=1\right)=\Gamma_{\mathrm{d,m}}\Big(\mathcal{C}_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{A}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{A}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{A}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{A}$}}\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{C}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{C}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{C}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{C}$}}},\mathcal{C}_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{A}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{A}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{A}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{A}$}}\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{B}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{B}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{B}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{B}$}}}=0\Big).

(38)

In contrast, the measurement rate is still dependent on the gain generated at mode A. However, for our system parameters, this dependence is minimal in the absence of added noise from the subsequent measurement chain, as illustrated in Fig.
8
(a). In this case, the measurement rate is reduced solely as a result of signal loss over the path C
\rightarrow
A
\rightarrow
B, which is minimized here due to the relatively large values of the beam-splitter cooperativities and the absence of thermal fluctuations. This contribution to the inefficiency becomes negligible as gain is increased. Conversely, the presence of substantial downstream added noise requires considerable amplification for
\Gamma_{\mathrm{meas}}
to approach its maximal value, which is again demonstrated in Fig.
8
(a).

Figure 8:
Theoretical amplifier performance as a function of squeezing strength.
Plotted as a function of
\lambda
is the
(a)
parasitic dephasing rate (pink) for both circulation directions
\phi=-\pi/2
(solid) and
\phi=\pi/2
(dashed),
(b)
measurement-induced dephasing (blue dotted-dashed) and measurement (purple) rates, and
(c)
inefficiency (dark blue), for fixed
\phi=-\pi/2
and
\bar{n}_{\mathrm{rest}}=0
(dashed) or
\bar{n}_{\mathrm{rest}}=5
(solid). Vertical dashed gray lines show correspondence between squeezing strength and reflection gain for the values
\mathcal{G}_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{B}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{B}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{B}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{B}$}}\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{B}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{B}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{B}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{B}$}}}=0,10
, and
20
dB. Other parameters are identical to those in Fig.
7
, including the fixed drive strength
\varepsilon/2\pi=20
MHz.

The improvements in the measurement rate due to amplification necessarily come at the expense of increased parasitic dephasing. Choosing the ideal circulation direction to favor C
\rightarrow
A scattering (
\phi=-\pi/2
), in accordance with Eq. (
34
), reduces the parasitic dephasing by one to two orders of magnitude in comparison to the opposing direction where A
\rightarrow
C scattering is dominant (
\phi=+\pi/2
), as shown in Fig.
8
(b). However, even for the favorable configuration, the finite bandwidth of the nonreciprocal interference provides only partial protection to mode C from the squeezed noise generated in mode A. As a result, even for the ideal configuration, there is residual
\Gamma_{\mathrm{d,p}}
present that scales with gain.

V.2
Measurement efficiency

To quantify the tradeoff of increasing both the measurement rate and the parasitic dephasing with gain, we numerically simulate the measurement efficiency
\eta
as a function of the squeezing interaction strength. Fig.
8
(c) presents the results for the inefficiency,
1-\eta
, for
\bar{n}_{\mathrm{rest}}=0
and 5 photons of downstream added noise, along with a fixed drive strength of
\varepsilon/2\pi=20
MHz. At high gain, the system is rendered insensitive to downstream added noise, and the inefficiency converges to a value below
4\%
for each value of
\bar{n}_{\mathrm{rest}}
. Notably, for the case of
\bar{n}_{\mathrm{rest}}=0
, the inefficiency reaches a minimum at
\lambda/2\pi=6.29
MHz (
\mathcal{G}_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{B}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{B}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{B}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{B}$}}\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{B}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{B}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{B}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{B}$}}}=11.7
dB). Beyond this squeezing strength, reductions in the inefficiency that come from increased robustness to internal losses are gradually overshadowed by the increased parasitic dephasing of the qubit.

The degree to which the measurement-induced dephasing overwhelms the parasitic dephasing is determined in large part by the drive amplitude
\varepsilon
. This can be seen in the numerical simulation of the inefficiency as a function of drive strength shown in Fig.
9
. For a fixed gain of
\mathcal{G}_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{B}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{B}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{B}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{B}$}}\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{B}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{B}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{B}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{B}$}}}=20
dB (
\lambda=9.83
MHz), increasing
\varepsilon
will decrease the inefficiency to an asymptotic value dependent on
\bar{n}_{\mathrm{rest}}
, where the condition
\Gamma_{\mathrm{d,m}}\gg\Gamma_{\mathrm{d,p}}
is met. In this regime, we may assume negligible parasitic dephasing, allowing us to obtain an approximate analytic expression for the measurement efficiency:

\displaystyle\eta\approx

\displaystyle\,\frac{\mathcal{C}_{bc}}{\mathcal{C}_{bc}+1}

(39)

\displaystyle\times\frac{(-1+\mathcal{C}_{ab}+\mathcal{C}_{\lambda})^{2}}{4\mathcal{C}_{ab}\mathcal{C}_{\lambda}+(2\bar{n}_{\mathrm{rest}}+1)(1+\mathcal{C}_{ab}-\mathcal{C}_{\lambda})^{2}}.

An upper bound on the efficiency can be found by saturating the critical stability criterion from Eq. (
37
), resulting in

\lim_{\mathcal{G}_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{B}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{B}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{B}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{B}$}}\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{B}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{B}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{B}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{B}$}}}\rightarrow\infty}\eta\approx\frac{\mathcal{C}_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{B}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{B}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{B}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{B}$}}\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{C}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{C}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{C}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{C}$}}}}{\mathcal{C}_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{B}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{B}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{B}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{B}$}}\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{C}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{C}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{C}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{C}$}}}+1}\times\frac{\mathcal{C}_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{A}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{A}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{A}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{A}$}}\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{B}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{B}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{B}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{B}$}}}}{\mathcal{C}_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{A}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{A}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{A}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{A}$}}\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{B}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{B}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{B}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{B}$}}}+1}.

(40)

As expected, in the infinite gain limit, the efficiency becomes independent of
\bar{n}_{\mathrm{rest}}
, and is principally limited by the beam-splitter cooperativities that can be achieved. Using the established device parameters and cooperativities, Eq. (
40
) provides an upper efficiency estimate of
\eta<97.5\%
.

This analysis has so far assumed that we are operating within the dispersive regime, however, increasing the drive strength will eventually lead to a breakdown of this model as spurious transmon state transitions become increasingly likely
Blais et al. 2004
;
Koch et al. 2007
;
Sank et al. 2016
;
Dumas et al. 2024
;
Khezri et al. 2023
;
Nesterov and Pechenezhskiy 2024
;
Connolly et al. 2025
. For the drive strength of
\varepsilon/2\pi=20
MHz used in Fig.
8
, the intracavity photon population for mode C is in the range of
\langle\hat{c}^{\dagger}\hat{c}\rangle\in[3.51,3.73]
photons for
\lambda/2\pi\in[0,10.84]
MHz, safely below the critical photon number regardless of the gain. For the amplifier under consideration, a range of drive strengths around
\varepsilon/2\pi=20
MHz are therefore weak enough to remain QND in most cases, yet strong enough such that
\Gamma_{\mathrm{d,m}}\gg\Gamma_{\mathrm{d,p}}
.

V.3
Limits to the efficiency

In Sec.
II.3
, we detailed two conditions for a high measurement efficiency. The second of these conditions demands that
\Gamma_{\mathrm{meas}}/\Gamma_{\mathrm{d,m}}\rightarrow 1
.

The approximate expression for the efficiency in Eq. (
40
) would appear to indicate that this condition can be met simply by increasing the unconstrained beam-splitter cooperativities
\mathcal{C}_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{B}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{B}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{B}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{B}$}}\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{C}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{C}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{C}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{C}$}}}
and
\mathcal{C}_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{A}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{A}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{A}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{A}$}}\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{B}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{B}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{B}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{B}$}}}
, allowing for an efficiency that comes arbitrarily close to unity. This improvement in the efficiency stems from the coherent processes overwhelming dissipation within the readout network, as discussed in Sec.
III
. However, apart from the experimental difficulties in achieving large parametric coupling rates, sufficiently large cooperativities also fundamentally curtail the ability of this system to meet the first condition from Sec.
II.3
, namely
\Gamma_{\mathrm{d,m}}\gg\Gamma_{\mathrm{d,p}}
, and so will eventually reduce the efficiency. The reason for this behavior is given here, with supporting calculations provided in Appendix
C.3
.

Figure 9:
Theoretical amplifier performance as a function of drive strength
. The inefficiency is plotted as a function of
\varepsilon
for
\bar{n}_{\mathrm{rest}}=0
(dashed) or
\bar{n}_{\mathrm{rest}}=5
(solid). A vertical dashed gray line represents a reasonable working point of
\varepsilon/2\pi=20
MHz corresponding to an intracavity C mode occupancy of 3.73 photons. Other parameters are identical to those in Fig.
7
, including the fixed squeezing strength
\lambda/2\pi=9.83
MHz (
\mathcal{G}_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{B}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{B}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{B}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{B}$}}\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{B}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{B}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{B}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{B}$}}}=20
dB).

In the three-mode readout network, each beam-splitter interaction between mode B and modes A and C increases the effective linewidths of the latter modes, providing additional damping,
\gamma_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{K}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{K}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{K}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{K}$}},\mathrm{eff}}=\gamma_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{K}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{K}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{K}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{K}$}}}(1+\mathcal{C}_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{B}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{B}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{B}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{B}$}}\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{K}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{K}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{K}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{K}$}}})
where K=A,C. For the AB interaction, increasing the effective A mode damping improves the system stability according to Eq. (
37
), and hence a commensurate increase in squeezing strength is required to retain a constant gain
\mathcal{G}_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{B}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{B}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{B}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{B}$}}\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{B}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{B}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{B}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{B}$}}}
. This directly leads to an increase in parasitic dephasing, while the measurement-induced dephasing rate remains constant, as established in Eq. (
38
). Additionally, the required squeezing will eventually become so large that the system becomes unstable even for finite values of
\mathcal{G}_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{B}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{B}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{B}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{B}$}}\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{B}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{B}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{B}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{B}$}}}
. Suitably large
\mathcal{C}_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{A}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{A}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{A}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{A}$}}\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{B}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{B}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{B}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{B}$}}}
therefore prevents the amplifier from fully combating
\bar{n}_{\mathrm{rest}}
.

For the BC interaction, the enhanced C mode damping overwhelms its dispersive coupling to the qubit when
\gamma_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{C}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{C}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{C}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{C}$}},\mathrm{eff}}\gg 2\chi_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{C}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{C}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{C}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{C}$}}}
. In this regime, analogous to the case of
\gamma\gg 2\chi
for a single-mode dispersive measurement, the separation between the pointer states becomes vanishingly small, reducing
\Gamma_{\mathrm{meas}}
and
\Gamma_{\mathrm{d,m}}
in the process. In comparison, the excess noise from the amplified intracavity state remains considerable. Therefore, for a fixed
\chi_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{C}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{C}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{C}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{C}$}}}
, an arbitrarily large value of
\mathcal{C}_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{B}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{B}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{B}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{B}$}}\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{C}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{C}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{C}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{C}$}}}
also overshadows the measurement process with parasitic dephasing.

The above complications limit the efficiency and render Eqs. (
39
) and (
40
) inapplicable for large cooperativities in the device considered here. However, despite these fundamental limitations, we have shown the theoretical promise of record-breaking efficiency for a reasonable extension of the experimentally-realized interferometer in Sec.
IV.2
.

VI
Conclusions and Outlook

In this paper, we have presented a general framework to quantify qubit measurement and backaction metrics in a multimode readout system. While existing methods allowed for the computation of the measurement rate, establishing quantitative understanding of qubit backaction from the coupled readout system required an extension of existing theoretical methods.

We then applied this theoretical framework to inform and understand the experimental implementation of a superconducting qubit dispersively coupled to a three-mode, parametric readout network. Readout network operations of increasing complexity were experimentally studied to extract system parameters and vet the theory, leading to a quantitative characterization of qubit measurement and backaction with a nonreciprocal interferometric readout. The inclusion of gain was theoretically explored, demonstrating the promise for efficient and low-backaction superconducting qubit readout in future experiments.

In addition to the experimental inclusion of gain, the work presented here suggests some natural improvements and extensions. For instance, favorable properties such as larger bandwidth for both gain and isolation can be achieved by incorporating additional modes and interactions
Roy et al. 2015
;
Kaufman et al. 2023
;
Abdo et al. 2025
. To this end, recent work has demonstrated the analytical and algorithmic-based synthesis of the linear response of coupled mode networks
Naaman and Aumentado 2022
;
Landgraf et al. 2025
, potentially accelerating the discovery of multimode systems with favorable qubit readout attributes. These methods, in conjunction with the theory presented here, could be integrated into a comprehensive workflow for readout network design.

While extra modes and interactions can produce favorable readout network attributes, they also augment the system’s complexity. The necessity of additional pumps particularly burdens the experimental operation of these devices through pump-induced frequency shifts and spurious mixing processes. Some of this complexity may be removed by employing resonant coupling, as opposed to beam-splitter couplings, for a subset of the inter-mode interactions
Kwende et al. 2023
. In addition to aiding the realizability of more complex devices, this strategy could also simplify the readout network presented in this work.

While this work focused on the readout of a single qubit, the theoretical framework presented is extensible to multi-qubit readout under some reasonable constraints. In addition to the requirements presented in Sec.
II
, the inter-qubit interactions remain
\hat{\sigma}_{z}
in nature, and the measurement must occur well within the relaxation time of each qubit. By similar argument, the phase space method could be extended to the readout of multilevel systems (qudits), with analogous constraints imposed to those of a multi-qubit readout.

Lastly, while this work emphasizes readout in a continuous regime, the dynamical equations constructed in Sec.
II
equally apply to time-domain readout protocols. These protocols, an example of which is proposed in Ref.
Bello et al. 2026
, are of interest as they allow for backaction that is principally determined by idle coupling between the readout cavity and the subsystem of the readout network with gain, or unintentional dispersive interactions. In a multimode parametric system, both of these can be suppressed to a high degree. A quantitative study of such a qubit readout performed with pulsed parametric interactions is an interesting subject for future exploration.

VII

The authors thank Massachusetts Institute of Technology Lincoln Laboratory for providing the TWPA. A.M. and L.O. acknowledge funding by the Deutsche Forschungsgemeinschaft through the project 163436311-SFB 910 and the Emmy Noether program (Grant No. ME 4863/1-1). F. L. and B. T. M. thank Jose Aumentado and John Teufel for their critical contributions to the design and troubleshooting of the experiment.

Appendix A
General theory: supporting expressions

A.1
The Wigner phase-space formulation

In order to efficiently convert the dynamical equation for a Gaussian operator to a system of differential equations for its moments, we first perform a Wigner transformation to convert the dynamical equation to a PDE in phase space. To simplify this process, we use the fact that the product between operators is replaced by an associative and non-commutative “star product” of QPDs in phase space. For the Wigner representation this is the Moyal product, which may be defined as

f(\hat{\bm{r}})g(\hat{\bm{r}})\xrightarrow{\text{Wigner}}W_{\!f}(\bm{r})\star W_{\!g}(\bm{r})=W_{\!f}(\bm{r})\exp\left[\frac{i\hbar}{2}\overleftarrow{\partial r}_{j}\Omega_{jk}\overrightarrow{\partial r}_{k}\right]W_{\!g}(\bm{r}),

(41)

where
W_{\!f}(\bm{r})
and
W_{\!g}(\bm{r})
are the Wigner QPDs associated with
f(\hat{\bm{r}})
and
g(\hat{\bm{r}})
, respectively. Here,
\partial r_{j}\vcentcolon=\partial/\partial r_{j}
is shorthand for the partial derivative operator with respect to the phase-space coordinate
r_{j}
, where the arrows above indicate that these derivatives are directional. Lastly,
\Omega_{jk}=-\Omega_{kj}
denotes a matrix element of
\bm{\Omega}
. Examples of
\bm{r}
and the associated
\bm{\Omega}
may be found in Eqs. (
8
) and (
9
) in the main text.

From this definition, the Wigner QPD for an operator
f(\hat{\bm{r}})
is
W_{\!f}(\bm{r})=f(\bm{r}\star)
. The linear operator
\hat{r}_{j}
therefore transforms to the phase-space symbol
r_{j}
. Additionally defining the Wigner transform of an operator
\hat{\varrho}
(not necessarily Hermitian) as
W(\bm{r})
, the dynamical equation for
\hat{\varrho}
may now be transformed to a phase-space PDE by treating the exponential in Eq. (
41
) as a power series. Taking
\hbar=1
, the coherent commutator terms in the dynamical equation then transform as follows

-i[{\hat{r}_{j}},{\hat{\varrho}}]\rightarrow-\partial r_{l}\Omega_{lj}W(\bm{r}),\qquad-i[{\hat{r}_{j}\hat{r}_{k}},{\hat{\varrho}}]\rightarrow-\partial r_{l}\left(\Omega_{lk}r_{j}+\Omega_{lj}r_{k}\right)W(\bm{r}),

(42)

while anti-commutator terms transform as

\{{\hat{r}_{j}},{\hat{\varrho}}\}\rightarrow 2r_{j}W(\bm{r}),\qquad\{{\hat{r}_{j}\hat{r}_{k}},{\hat{\varrho}}\}\rightarrow\left(2r_{j}r_{k}+i\Omega_{jk}+\frac{1}{2}\Omega_{lj}\Omega_{km}\partial r_{l}\partial r_{m}\right)W(\bm{r}).

(43)

The so-called jump terms take the form

\displaystyle\hat{r}_{k}\hat{\varrho}\hat{r}_{j}\rightarrow\left(r_{j}r_{k}+\frac{i}{2}\Omega_{jk}+\frac{i}{2}\partial r_{l}\left(\Omega_{lj}r_{k}-\Omega_{lk}r_{j}\right)-\frac{1}{4}\Omega_{lj}\Omega_{km}\partial r_{l}\partial r_{m}\right)W(\bm{r})

(44)

which, combined with the relevant anti-commutator, allows for dissipation terms to be transformed as

\displaystyle\hat{r}_{k}\hat{\varrho}\hat{r}_{j}-\frac{1}{2}\{{\hat{r}_{j}\hat{r}_{k}},{\hat{\varrho}}\}\rightarrow\frac{1}{2}\Big(i\partial r_{l}\left(\Omega_{lj}r_{k}-\Omega_{lk}r_{j}\right)-\Omega_{lj}\Omega_{km}\partial r_{l}\partial r_{m}\Big)W(\bm{r}).

(45)

With these relations, the dynamical equation for
\hat{\rho}_{eg}
in Eq. (
15
) may be expressed as a differential equation for
W_{eg}(\bm{r})
. For this, we start by writing the quadratic Hamiltonians
\hat{H}_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{R}$}}}
and
\hat{H}_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{Q}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{Q}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{Q}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{Q}$}}\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{R}$}}}
from Eqs. (
12
) and (
13
) in the form
\hat{H}=\hat{r}_{j}H_{jk}\hat{r}_{k}/2+h_{j}\hat{r}_{j}+h
, where
H_{jk}=H_{kj}
. Eq. (
15
),
\dot{\hat{\rho}}_{eg}=\mathcal{L}_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{R}$}}}(\hat{\rho}_{eg})-i\{{\hat{H}_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{Q}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{Q}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{Q}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{Q}$}}\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{R}$}}}},{\hat{\rho}_{eg}}\}
, may now be expressed as:

\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial t}W_{eg}(\bm{r})=\bigg[-2ih_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{Q}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{Q}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{Q}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{Q}$}}\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{R}$}}}-\partial r_{j}\Omega_{jl}(h_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{R}$}}})_{l}-2ir_{j}(h_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{Q}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{Q}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{Q}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{Q}$}}\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{R}$}}})_{j}

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{2}\partial r_{j}\Omega_{jl}\left(\frac{i}{2}(H_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{Q}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{Q}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{Q}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{Q}$}}\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{R}$}}})_{lm}+\real[\Gamma_{lm}]\right)\Omega_{mk}\partial r_{k}

\displaystyle-ir_{j}(H_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{Q}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{Q}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{Q}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{Q}$}}\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{R}$}}})_{jk}r_{k}-\partial r_{j}\Omega_{jl}\Big((H_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{R}$}}})_{lk}+\mathrm{Im}[\Gamma_{lk}]\Big)r_{k}\bigg]W_{eg}(\bm{r}).

(46)

The real and imaginary parts of
\Gamma_{jk}
are inserted by identifying that
\Gamma_{jk}=\Gamma^{*}_{kj}
. Time evolution equations may also be obtained for the intracavity pointer state Wigner functions, denoted
W_{\sigma\sigma}(\bm{r})
for
\hat{\rho}_{\sigma\sigma}
where
\sigma=e,g
. The reduced Lindblad master equation for these intracavity pointer states,
\dot{\hat{\rho}}_{\sigma\sigma}=\mathcal{L}_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{R}$}}}(\hat{\rho}_{\sigma\sigma})-i\braket{\sigma|\hat{\sigma}_{z}|\sigma}[{\hat{H}_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{Q}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{Q}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{Q}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{Q}$}}\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{R}$}}}},{\hat{\rho}_{\sigma\sigma}}]
, maps to the following PDE:

\frac{\partial}{\partial t}W_{\sigma\sigma}(\bm{r})=\bigg[-\partial r_{j}\Omega_{jl}(h_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{R}$}}}+sh_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{Q}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{Q}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{Q}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{Q}$}}\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{R}$}}})_{l}-\frac{1}{2}\partial r_{j}\Omega_{jl}\real[\Gamma_{lm}]\Omega_{mk}\partial r_{k}-\partial r_{j}\Omega_{jl}\Big((H_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{R}$}}}+sH_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{Q}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{Q}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{Q}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{Q}$}}\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{R}$}}})_{lk}+\mathrm{Im}[\Gamma_{lk}]\Big)r_{k}\bigg]W_{\sigma\sigma}(\bm{r})

(47)

where
s=\braket{\sigma|\hat{\sigma}_{z}|\sigma}
yields
s=1
for
\sigma=e
, and
s=-1
for
\sigma=g
.

A.2
Dynamical equations for moments of the Wigner function

The Gaussian property of a Wigner QPD
W(\bm{r})
is preserved when
W(\bm{r})
evolves according to a PDE of the form

\frac{\partial}{\partial t}W(\bm{r})=\left[-g-\partial r_{j}d_{j}-r_{j}f_{j}+\frac{1}{2}\partial r_{j}C_{jk}\partial r_{k}-\frac{1}{2}r_{j}B_{jk}r_{k}-\partial r_{j}A_{jk}r_{k}\right]W(\bm{r}).

(48)

Fourier transforming the above PDE to the reciprocal phase space coordinates yields another PDE for the characteristic function of
W(\bm{r})
,
w(\bm{\xi})
, as follows

\frac{\partial}{\partial t}w(\bm{\xi})=\left[-g-i\xi_{j}d_{j}-i\partial\xi_{j}f_{j}-\frac{1}{2}\xi_{j}C_{jk}\xi_{k}+\frac{1}{2}\partial\xi_{j}B_{jk}\partial\xi_{k}+\xi_{j}A_{jk}\partial\xi_{k}\right]w(\bm{\xi}).

(49)

To derive the moment equations, the characteristic function for a Gaussian QPD is inserted into the above PDE. From Eq. (
16
), the characteristic function may be written as

w(\bm{\xi})=\exp\left[-\frac{1}{2}\Sigma_{jk}\xi_{j}\xi_{k}-i\mu_{k}\xi_{k}-\nu\right]

(50)

where
\Sigma_{jk}=\Sigma_{kj}
since the covariance matrix is symmetric. Evaluating the derivatives and collecting terms of identical order reveals the following differential equations for the individual moments,

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}\Sigma_{jk}

\displaystyle=A_{jl}\Sigma_{lk}+\Sigma_{jl}A^{T}_{lk}-\Sigma_{jl}B_{lm}\Sigma_{mk}+C_{jk},

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}\mu_{j}

\displaystyle=A_{jk}\mu_{k}-\Sigma_{jk}B_{kl}\mu_{l}+d_{j}-\Sigma_{jk}f_{k},

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}\nu

\displaystyle=g+f_{k}\mu_{k}+\mu_{j}B_{jk}\mu_{k}+\frac{1}{2}B_{jk}\Sigma_{kj},

(51)

which are identical to those in Eq. (
II.2
). The expressions in Eq. (
II.2
) may then be obtained by comparing terms in Eqs. (
A.1
) and (
48
). When using these equations to calculate the intracavity pointer states, Eq. (
47
) shows that only the following arrays are non-zero:

\bm{A}=\bm{\Omega}\left(\bm{H}_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{R}$}}}+s\bm{H}_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{Q}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{Q}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{Q}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{Q}$}}\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{R}$}}}+\mathrm{Im}[\bm{\Gamma}]\right),\qquad\bm{C}=-\bm{\Omega}\real[\bm{\Gamma}]\bm{\Omega},\qquad\bm{d}=\bm{\Omega}\left(\bm{h}_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{R}$}}}+s\bm{h}_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{Q}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{Q}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{Q}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{Q}$}}\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.8}{$\displaystyle\mathrm{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\textstyle\mathrm{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptstyle\mathrm{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{R}$}}}\right).

(52)

A.3
Heisenberg-Langevin equations

While the Heisenberg-Langevin equations (HLEs) are used to model the output fields of the readout network required to calculate the measurement rate, they may also be used to model the intracavity pointer states. These dynamics must therefore be consistent with those obtained from the Lindblad master equation, and hence the dynamical equations for the means and covariances obtained from the HLEs must be identical. In order to check that both formalisms yield the same intracavity dynamics, we start by deriving the HLEs following the approach of Ref.
Gardiner and Collett 1985
. We consider a system of
N
linear modes coupled to
M
independent baths, which are treated as noninteracting harmonic oscillators. The total Hamiltonian is written as
\hat{H}=\hat{H}_{\mathrm{s}}+\hat{H}_{\mathrm{b}}+\hat{H}_{\mathrm{sb}}
, where:

\hat{H}_{\mathrm{s}}=\frac{1}{2}\hat{\bm{r}}^{T}\bm{H}_{\mathrm{s}}\hat{\bm{r}}+\hat{\bm{r}}^{T}\bm{h}_{\mathbf{s}},\qquad\hat{H}_{\mathrm{b}}=\frac{1}{2}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\omega\hat{\bm{r}}_{\mathrm{b}}^{T}(\omega)\hat{\bm{r}}_{\mathrm{b}}(\omega)\,d\omega,\qquad\hat{H}_{\mathrm{sb}}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\hat{\bm{r}}^{T}\bm{H}_{\mathrm{sb}}\hat{\bm{r}}_{\mathrm{b}}(\omega)\,d\omega,

(53)

Here,
(\bm{H}_{\mathrm{s}})^{T}=\bm{H}_{\mathrm{s}}\in\mathbb{R}^{2N\times 2N}
,
\bm{h}_{\mathbf{s}}\in\mathbb{R}^{2N}
, and
\bm{H}_{\mathrm{sb}}\in\mathbb{R}^{2N\times 2M}
. The Markov approximation has already been applied, which renders the system-bath coupling independent of frequency. In the above,
\hat{\bm{r}}
is the vector of
2N
system quadrature operators, while
\hat{\bm{r}}_{\mathrm{b}}(\omega)
is the vector of
2M
bath quadrature operators at the frequency
\omega
, which obey
[{\hat{r}_{\mathrm{b},j}(\omega)},{\hat{r}_{\mathrm{b},k}(\omega^{\prime})}]=i\Omega_{jk}\delta(\omega-\omega^{\prime})
. The Heisenberg equation for
\hat{\bm{r}}_{\mathrm{b}}(\omega)
is formally solved and substituted into the dynamical equation for
\hat{\bm{r}}
to obtain the HLEs:

\frac{d}{dt}\hat{\bm{r}}(t)=\bm{A}\hat{\bm{r}}(t)+\bm{\Omega}_{2N}\bm{h}_{\mathbf{s}}+\bm{\Omega}_{2N}\bm{H}_{\mathrm{sb}}\hat{\bm{r}}_{\mathrm{in}}(t)\quad\text{where}\quad\bm{A}=\bm{\Omega}_{2N}\!\left(\bm{H}_{\mathrm{s}}+\frac{1}{2}\bm{H}_{\mathrm{sb}}\bm{\Omega}_{2M}\bm{H}_{\mathrm{sb}}^{T}\right).

(54)

Here,
\bm{\Omega}_{2N}
and
\bm{\Omega}_{2M}
are the matrix representation of the symplectic forms for the system and bath operators, respectively. The vector
\hat{\bm{r}}_{\mathrm{in}}(t)
contains the
2M
input-field quadrature operators, defined as

\hat{r}_{\mathrm{in},j}(t)\vcentcolon=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\!\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\!\!\left(e^{-i\omega(t-t_{0})\bm{\Omega}}\right)_{\!\!jk}\!\!\hat{r}_{\mathrm{b},k}(\omega;t_{0})\,d\omega=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\!\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\!\!\Big(\delta_{jk}\cos\bm{(}\omega(t-t_{0})\bm{)}+\Omega_{jk}\sin\bm{(}\omega(t-t_{0})\bm{)}\!\Big)\hat{r}_{\mathrm{b},k}(\omega;t_{0})\,d\omega,

(55)

where
\hat{r}_{\mathrm{b},j}(\omega;t_{0})
is the value of
\hat{r}_{\mathrm{b},j}(\omega)
at an initial time
t_{0}<t
. The input fields satisfy the canonical commutation relations,
[{\hat{r}_{\mathrm{in},j}(t)},{\hat{r}_{\mathrm{in},k}(t^{\prime})}]=i\Omega_{jk}\delta(t-t^{\prime})
, and are assumed to be Gaussian noise processes, with first and second moments given by

\langle\hat{r}_{\mathrm{in},j}(t)\rangle=\mu_{\mathrm{in},j},\qquad\frac{1}{2}\langle\hat{r}_{\mathrm{in},j}(t)\hat{r}_{\mathrm{in},k}(t^{\prime})+\hat{r}_{\mathrm{in},k}(t^{\prime})\hat{r}_{\mathrm{in},j}(t)\rangle-\langle\hat{r}_{\mathrm{in},j}(t)\rangle\langle\hat{r}_{\mathrm{in},k}(t^{\prime})\rangle=(\bar{S}_{\mathrm{in}})_{jk}\delta(t-t^{\prime}).

(56)

With these correlators, and the HLEs in Eq. (
54
), we can now derive dynamical equations for the intracavity moments. For the means,
\mu_{j}\vcentcolon=\langle\hat{r}_{j}\rangle
, this is simply the expectation value of the HLE for
\hat{r}_{j}
. For the covariances,
\Sigma_{jk}\vcentcolon=\frac{1}{2}\langle\hat{r}_{j}\hat{r}_{k}+\hat{r}_{k}\hat{r}_{j}\rangle-\langle\hat{r}_{j}\rangle\langle\hat{r}_{k}\rangle
, we must use the fact the HLEs are consistent with calculus
Gardiner and Collett 1985
,
\frac{d}{dt}(\hat{r}_{j}\hat{r}_{k})=(\frac{d}{dt}\hat{r}_{j})\hat{r}_{k}+\hat{r}_{j}(\frac{d}{dt}\hat{r}_{k})
. When evaluating terms of the form
